*Declined: `"CC BY 4.0"`.* A third-party audit (`strale-io/strale`) asserts "License: CC BY 4.0 (or equivalent), confirmed in coverage matrix". **Nothing on Bolagsverket's pages supports it**, and repeating a secondary source's licence claim about a primary publisher is exactly the class of error the research rules exist to prevent — it would put a specific, legally meaningful name on every Swedish report and on `/v1/countries`. *Declined: `"Public domain"` or `"Open data"`.* Both assert more than the prose does; the prose bounds the permission by personal-data and secrecy law, which is not a public-domain dedication. *Declined: leaving it empty.* `license` is on every report for a reason — provenance is the product — and "we don't know" is not what is true here: we know the permission, we just have no name for it.

**We cite the source anyway.** Attribution is "sometimes" required and never specified, so the safe reading — and the one already in this codebase's grain — is to attribute unconditionally: `source = "Bolagsverket (bolagsverket.se)"` on every report, and `rules_markdown()` quotes both Swedish sentences verbatim. A citation is what makes an agent's answer checkable, which is the reason we would do it even if nobody asked.
Reason: the failure mode here is not under-claiming, it is a confident licence name that a lawyer downstream relies on. Quoting the regime is the only statement that is true, and saying out loud that no licence name exists is what stops a reader from assuming we simply forgot to fill it in.
Applies to tasks: T26a, T26b, T26c

### D-039 — A Swedish sole trader's identifier *is* a personnummer: the note, and the limit of what a country module can do about it
Date: 2026-09-05
Decision: `registries/se/mapping.py` appends a personal-data `notes` sentence when the record is a sole trader — detected from `organisationsidentitet.typ.kod` ∈ `{PERSONNUMMER, SAMORDNINGSNUMMER, GDNUMMER, DODSBO}` **or** `organisationsform.kod == "E"`. It mirrors T20's Norwegian ENK note and goes further in one respect, because Sweden's case is worse: brreg publishes an ENK's *name* and often a home address, while Bolagsverket's identifier **is itself** a national personal identity number.

The sentence states that this is an *enskild näringsidkare*, that the identifier is the proprietor's personnummer, that the registered name and address are often the person's own, that the record therefore contains personal data about a natural person — and that **Bolagsverket takes the same view**: its read operations are POSTs rather than GETs precisely so the identitetsbeteckning does not reach URLs, access logs and `Referer` headers. Quoting the register's own behaviour makes the caveat a fact rather than our opinion. `CompanyReport.id` carries the number once, because the caller supplied it; **no `notes` sentence ever repeats it**, and `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §14 test 93 asserts that.

**Three consequences beyond the note, all of them rules rather than advice.** The module never converts the lookup to a `GET` with the identifier in a query string, however much easier that would make caching. `CompanyReport.previous_names`, `notes` and every derived string are checked for the identifier before shipping. And **no production sole-trader payload may ever be committed as a test fixture** — Bolagsverket's test-environment data is synthetic and safe to record, production data is a real person's name, personnummer and home address in a public MIT repository.

**And the part the module cannot fix, recorded here because a decision that names its own limit is worth more than one that implies it is complete.** `api/main.py` and the MCP surface pass the caller's raw `id` to `core/log.py::log_call` as `query`, which writes it into the `calls` table. For `NO` and `GB` that is a company number; for `SE` it is, on every sole-trader lookup, a personnummer — put back into exactly the kind of log Bolagsverket designed its API to keep it out of. **`registries/se/` has no way to intervene**: the logging happens in the surface, around the country module. This is a `core/` + `api/` finding, it is carried as F1 in `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §0, and `tasks/T26.md` makes it the orchestrator's to take to Kim rather than an edit to make. The smallest shape that would close it — recorded so the next architect does not re-derive it — is a `Registry` class attribute (`id_may_be_personal: ClassVar[bool] = False`) that the two call sites consult, storing a salted hash or nothing instead of the raw query, defaulted so that no existing module is edited: the same mechanism D-028(2) reaches for when it gives person-bearing cache kinds their own TTL.
Reason: D-028 ruled that officers turn a company-data product into a personal-data product and set four preconditions before that may happen. Sweden gets there without any of it — no new endpoint, no new field, no `include=[...]` — simply because a sole trader's company identifier *is* their personal one. The note is the cheapest half and it ships now; the logging half is a shared-surface change, and the honest thing is to name it, size it, and not do it under a task that forbids it.
Applies to tasks: T26a, T26b, T26e, and a finding for Kim (`core/log.py`, `api/main.py`)

### D-040 — An identifier that may be a person's is never written to the usage log: `Registry.id_may_be_personal`, and the stored `query` is nothing, not a hash
Date: 2026-09-06
Decision: closes finding F1 (`SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §0, D-039's last paragraph). Six parts.

**(a) One flag on the contract.** `core/registry.py` gains `id_may_be_personal: ClassVar[bool] = False` beside `requires_api_key`, documented as: *"True when an identifier this registry accepts can be a natural person's national identity number (Sweden: a sole trader's organisationsnummer is their personnummer). The surfaces consult it before logging; the module does not."* `registries/se/__init__.py` sets it `True`. `NO`, `GB` and the `XX` stub keep the default. Every future country spec must state the flag explicitly in its §1 — Denmark's (T16) in particular, because "enkeltmandsvirksomhed" sounds like Sweden's case and is not: a CVR number is not a CPR number, and the spec must say which it is rather than let the default decide.

**(b) One choke point per surface, one pure helper in `core/`.** `core/registry.py` gains `loggable_query(country: str | None, query: str | None) -> str | None`: resolves the registry (stubs included; an unresolvable country returns `query` unchanged, because a country we do not serve cannot be Sweden), returns `None` when the flag is set, else `query`. `api/main.py::_record` and `mcp/server.py::_call_context` call it — nowhere else, so no route or tool changes. The two places that log before they know the country are ruled explicitly: the D-031 `fetch` alias logs `country` as the prefix it parsed out of `"{COUNTRY}:{identifier}"` and `query` as the bare identifier, both through the helper (a malformed id that has no prefix logs as today); the D-031 `search` alias runs its free text through the helper with the country `_derive_country` produced, because an agent that types a personnummer into `search` has typed a personnummer, and the D-031 design invites exactly that ("a name, a national identifier, or a name plus a country"). *Clarified at implementation (same day, T28):* the country token is not the only way a personnummer reaches `search` — with no token, D-031(c)'s identifier short-circuit validates the text against every registry and looks it up wherever it validates, so a bare twelve-digit string performs an SE lookup. The rule therefore has two triggers: the derived country's flag, **or** any flagged registry validating the text as an identifier. Blanket and by flag, as (c) and (d) require; the log's `country` stays whatever `_derive_country` produced.

**(c) The stored value is `NULL`. Not a salted hash, not a shape marker.** A Swedish personnummer has on the order of 10⁸–10⁹ possible values; a hash whose salt lives beside the database is reversed by enumeration in minutes, and a *secret* salt is a second secret to provision, rotate and back up whose only consumer would be a `top_queries` column that a human can no longer read. `top_queries` exists to show a person which companies agents ask about; a hash there is noise, and `None` there is already handled (`core/stats.py:107` skips empty queries). Every aggregate the dashboard actually shows — by country, operation, outcome, cache hit, user-agent class — has its own column and is untouched. And `legal/privacy.md` §"Your rights" promises deletion on request: a value never stored needs no deletion. *Declined: a shape marker such as `"12 digits"`.* It is a fact about a person (sole trader, not company) in exchange for a sole-trader count nobody has asked for; if the dashboard wants that count it is a separate decision with its own column. *Declined: deciding per record from the response's `organisationsidentitet.typ.kod`.* The log line is written in a `finally` around the call and `validate_company_id` never has a response; a rule that depends on the answer cannot protect the question. Blanket, by country.

**(d) `NULL` for every identifier-bearing operation on a flagged country, including ten-digit ones.** D-032 refused to distinguish a ten-digit personnummer from an organisationsnummer because the rule is unsourced; the log must not smuggle that distinction back in. The cost is that Swedish `AB` lookups vanish from `top_queries`. That is the trade, and it is the right one: the column is a convenience, the log is kept "for the life of the service" (`legal/privacy.md`).

**(e) The URL is the other copy, and it is a deploy setting.** D-007's REST route carries the identifier in the path, so `GET /v1/SE/company/<personnummer>` is what a Swedish sole-trader lookup looks like on the wire. Nothing here changes that (an MCP call carries it in a POST body, as Bolagsverket does). But uvicorn writes an access line per request by default and the `Dockerfile` CMD does not turn it off, so the path — with the number — would be written to Railway's application log stream beside our own usage log. The CMD gains `--no-access-log`: the access log duplicates `calls` with an IP address added, and the IP address is the one thing `legal/privacy.md` already says we choose not to keep, so nothing is lost that `/v1/stats` does not already show. Railway's edge logs are outside our control and `legal/privacy.md` already says so.

**(f) The privacy notice changes with the code, in the same task.** `legal/privacy.md` "Your request" currently says the identifier is written to the usage log. It gains one sentence: for countries where the identifier can be a natural person's number (today Sweden), the identifier itself is not stored; the country, route, time, outcome and `User-Agent` still are. The retention line is unchanged. No backfill is needed — Sweden is not deployed, so no production row can carry a Swedish query; T26d's go-live checklist gets a one-line `SELECT count(*) FROM calls WHERE country = 'SE'` on the production file before the SE build ships, expected `0`.

**Considered and left alone: the cache.** `core/cache.py` holds the record — and, for a sole trader, the person's name and address — under a key that contains the identifier, for D-006's TTL. That is not a log: it is bounded, it is what serving the request requires, and D-028(2) already has the mechanism (a shorter TTL for person-bearing kinds) if the T16/T28 work finds a reason to use it. Recorded so the next reader does not mistake the omission for an oversight.
Reason: D-039 named the limit of a country module — the logging happens in the surface, around it — and sized the fix. This entry takes the two decisions D-039 deliberately left open (hash or nothing; where the check lives) and rules them, so that a Sonnet can implement without inventing policy. The test of the shape is the one D-001 always applies: a fourth country with a personal identifier sets one attribute and edits nothing else. Not authorised by this entry — Kim lifts the `core/` freeze for it ("do F1"); `tasks/T28.md` is the brief.
**Addendum, 2026-09-06, after the review of T28 (REVIEW.md "T26f + T28 + T29").** Four things, recorded so they are not rediscovered as bugs. *(i) A third trigger for the `search` alias.* Validating the whole text was not enough — `"194009272719 AB"`, `"orgnr 194009272719"` and `"NO 194009272719"` all logged the number, the last because an explicit token narrows the candidates to one unflagged registry. The rule is now: every maximal alphanumeric run of the query (plus the whole text and the remainder) is validated against every *live flagged* registry, and one hit stores `NULL`. Still blanket and by flag, never by shape; two residuals are deliberately left open — a number glued to letters with no separator, and a number inside a longer digit run — because closing them needs the shape scan (c) declined. *(ii) Two more sinks, both closed.* `api/errors.py`'s unhandled-exception line logged the concrete request path — the same `GET /v1/SE/company/<number>` (e) turned the access log off for — and now logs the method plus the route template; `core/cache.py`'s two failure lines logged the cache key, whose last segment is the identifier, and now log the key's prefix only. "Considered and left alone: the cache" was about the contents; the key in a WARNING line is a log, and (c) applies. *(iii) What the flag protects.* It protects the country *asked about*, not the number *typed*: `lookup_company("NO", <personnummer>)` logs it in full, by design — (c) and (d) rule out guessing from shape, and a caller who sends a Swedish number to the Norwegian register has made an error we cannot see. *(iv) FastMCP logs full tool arguments at DEBUG.* Latent, not live; the SE go-live checklist (T26d) requires the deployed log level to be INFO or above, and `HUMAN_TODO.md` should not turn DEBUG on in production for any reason.
Applies to tasks: T28 (new), T30 (review fixes), T26d (go-live check: `SELECT count(*) FROM calls WHERE country = 'SE'` expected 0, and log level ≥ INFO), T16 (Denmark must state the flag), every future country spec §1

### D-041 — The financial year end is a published fact, not an assumption: `include=["filings"]` carries it with its own `SourceRef`, and a date computed from it is a different rung from one computed from a guess
Date: 2026-09-07
Decision: reading Bolagsverket's developer portal after Sweden shipped turns up a sourced factual error in `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §5.4 and in the running 0.3.0 build. Nine parts. Nothing here is authorised to start before R-5 (see (i)).

**(a) The correction, and it is shipped prose that is wrong.** `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §5.4.2, §5.4.3 and note N9 all assert that *"Bolagsverket's free dataset does not publish the financial year"*, and every Swedish deadline is computed from an assumed 31 December year end on the strength of it. **The claim is false.** The same free API, under the same credential and the same `vardefulla-datamangder:read` scope, exposes `POST /dokumentlista`, whose `DokumentlistaSvar_dokument_inner` carries `rapporteringsperiodTom` — the end of the reporting period of each filed annual report, i.e. the company's own financial year end — beside `registreringstidpunkt`, the moment it was filed. Both are declared `format: date` in the OpenAPI document (`~/research/registry-mcp/02-registers-landscape/02b-sweden-openapi.json`), and `tasks/T26-recon.md` line ~100 records a real item from the test workbook: `{"dokumentId":"02f54e4f-…_paket","filformat":"application/zip","rapporteringsperiodTom":"2022-12-31","registreringstidpunkt":"2023-06-27"}`. A *brutet räkenskapsår* is lawful in Sweden, so a company with an April year end is today told 30 June and 31 July when its own dates are four months later. Disclosing the assumption in N9 is honest and does not make the date right.

The false sentence lives in **seven shipped strings across four files**, and one of them is the tool description an agent reads before it calls anything: `registries/se/rules.py` (`_CALENDAR_YEAR_NOTE`, both `_APPLIES_BECAUSE` strings, and `rules_markdown()`'s "What this dataset does not publish"), `mcp/server.py:461`, `static/well-known/mcp/server-card.json` (the same `company_deadlines` description), and `README.md:150`. **Correcting the sentence is neither a `core/` change nor a shape change, and it does not wait for the rest of this entry** — "we do not read the document list that would show it" is true today, "the register does not publish it" is not. That is D-023(c) applied to Sweden: a gap we can close must not be described as a boundary we cannot. This half ships as soon as an implementer is free.

**(b) It is read on request, never by default, and the argument is `include=["filings"]` on *two* operations.** D-026(c)'s mechanism, third user, unamended: a closed set, validated against the country module's declared capabilities, an unknown value raising `bad_request` naming the allowed set, default `[]`. The one extension is that the argument goes on **`company_deadlines` as well as `lookup_company`**, because unlike the LEI (D-026(c)) and Peppol (D-029) this attachment *changes the answer of another operation*. An `include` reachable only from the lookup would make an agent that wants a correct deadline call the wrong tool to get one. `DeadlineReport` does **not** gain the block — the effect is visible in `applies_because` and in `notes`, and a caller who wants the history calls `lookup_company` (D-010: one shape per operation).

*Declined: a second call on every Swedish lookup.* Sweden's 60/min is the tightest budget in the project (`SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §1.5) and it is the *published* limit, not a runaway guard — the client already carries a token bucket because a modest agent loop reaches it legitimately. Doubling every lookup halves that headroom to buy a fact most callers never read; and R-1's batch cap is 50, so a default would turn one batch call into 100 upstream requests and a guaranteed >40-second queue. **`include` is therefore not accepted on R-1's batch path at all until a later entry rules otherwise** — an attachment that costs an upstream request per row does not belong behind a cap counted in rows.

*Declined: fetching it only on the deadline path, since only deadlines use it.* Two objections, either sufficient. It would return two contradicting documents for one entity at one moment — `lookup_company` saying the year end is unknown while `company_deadlines` says 30 April — which is exactly the ambiguity D-004's one-shape rule exists to prevent. And there is nowhere lawful to put the fetch: `core/registry.py::deadline_report` is **synchronous** and takes an already-fetched `CompanyReport`, so the second call would have to live in `api/main.py` and `mcp/server.py`, putting country-specific I/O in the surfaces — D-001's prohibition, and D-018's whole finding restated (`raw_for` was a synchronous read inside a method whose contract forbids I/O; a synchronous fetch inside a surface is worse).

*Declined: a sixth tool, and a `GET /v1/{country}/company/{id}/filings` sub-route.* D-024's count stands and this is not a capability an agent selects between; D-028(5) gave officers a sub-route because that block has a privacy story of its own that a caller may want to fetch alone, which is not true here. Revisit only if a caller asks.

**(c) It still gets a `SourceRef`, and the reason corrects D-026(c)'s own reasoning.** The company record and the document list come from one register, under one licence, on one credential. D-026(c)'s stated objection — *"a `lei` populated from GLEIF inside a document whose provenance block says Enhetsregisteret … is a provenance lie"* — does not reach this case, and if that were the whole rule, `rapporteringsperiodTom` could be a plain field. It is not the whole rule. D-026(c)'s operative sentence is the one before it: *"`CompanyReport` has one `source`, one `source_url`, one `license` and one `fetched_at`. Every field in it is therefore an assertion by that register under that licence **at that moment**."*

**The discipline is one *fetch*, one `SourceRef` — not one organisation.** Two round trips have two moments, two independent failure modes and two independent cache states: the record can be a 20-hour-old cache hit while the document list is fresh, or the reverse; the record can succeed while the list 500s. A plain field cannot say *this one value is a day older than the rest of the document*, cannot say *this one fetch failed while the rest succeeded*, and would put a value under a `fetched_at` that never applied to it. `SourceRef` says all three with the five names `CompanyReport` already uses, and it is used here **unamended**, which is the best available evidence that D-026(c) got the shape right.

Two consequences follow immediately and both are rules, not notes. **`published_deadlines` is not extended by a second fetch**, even though `PublishedDeadline` fits: its own docstring anticipates precisely Sweden's case (`due_date` *"`None` when the register names a period but no date — the country module may still be able to compute one from `period_end`"*), and the temptation to fill `kind="annual_accounts", due_date=None, period_end=<observed>` is real. It is refused because `published_deadlines` is a bare list on `CompanyReport` with no provenance of its own, so a second-fetch value landing there would inherit a `fetched_at` and a `cached` that are not its own — the exact defect (b) and (c) exist to prevent. D-018's field keeps one meaning: **dates the register published in the same document as the company record.** Denmark's `nyesteRegnskabsperiode` and Companies House's accounting reference date arrive that way and belong there; Sweden's does not. **And `CompanyReport.last_annual_accounts_year` stays `None` for Sweden permanently** — not `DEFERRED`. The year is in the block, where its provenance travels with it. Norway may fill the field because `sisteInnsendteAarsregnskap` is in the same payload; Sweden may not, because it is one round trip away. That asymmetry is the rule working, not the rule failing.

The two-level nullability is D-026(c)'s, restated for this block: **an absent block means "you did not ask, or the fetch failed"** (and a failed fetch appends a `notes` sentence saying so, never a present block with invented content); **a present block with `documents: []` means "Bolagsverket holds no filed annual report for this entity"**, which is a real and useful answer about a counterparty and must never be rendered as an absence.

**(d) The shape.** `core/models.py` gains two models; `CompanyReport` gains one field, `filings: FilingHistory | None = None`, always present per D-004, `null` on every country that is not asked.

```python
class FiledDocument(_Base):
    kind: str | None = None           # the Deadline slug this filing discharges: "annual_accounts"
    period_end: date | None = None    # rapporteringsperiodTom — the reporting period's last day
    period_start: date | None = None  # None for SE: Bolagsverket publishes no ...From
    filed_at: date | None = None      # registreringstidpunkt
    days_from_fee_point: int | None = None  # signed; negative = filed before it
    document_id: str | None = None    # opaque upstream handle; not fetchable through this API
    file_format: str | None = None    # "application/zip" — a MIME type despite the field name

class FilingHistory(_Base):
    documents: list[FiledDocument] = []
    financial_year_end: date | None = None   # carried verbatim; never a synthesised month-day
    provenance: SourceRef
    notes: list[str] = []
```

Three shape rulings inside that. **`financial_year_end` is a real `date`, carried verbatim** — the `period_end` of the most recently filed annual report, ties broken by the later `filed_at` — and never a month-day string or an `(int, int)` pair. It is *evidence of* the current financial year, not a statement of it: a company may relay its räkenskapsår, and the field description must say so. That is D-026(a)'s "carried verbatim, never constructed" applied to a date. **`period_start` exists on the model and is `None` for Sweden**, because `rapporteringsperiodTom` has no `...From` counterpart; Norway's Regnskapsregisteret publishes `regnskapsperiode: {fraDato, tilDato}` (D-023(c)) and will fill it, which is why the field is there at all. **`days_from_fee_point` is a signed integer and there is no boolean.** A `filed_late: bool` would be a verdict this dataset cannot support: ÅRL 8 kap. 6 §'s nine-month variant applies to companies the free dataset does not identify, and 8 kap. 3 §'s actual deadline runs from an AGM date nobody publishes, so the true answer is unknowable in both directions. An integer measured against a named datum is a measurement; a boolean is a legal conclusion. The field description must name the datum — seven months from `period_end`, ÅRL 8 kap. 6 § — and `None` whenever `period_end` or `filed_at` is missing. *Declined: `filed_late: bool | None`.* D-011's invariant and D-028(3)'s: a field whose job is to distinguish two states must not have a third that silently means both.

**(e) A deadline computed from a published period end is the second rung of a four-rung ladder, and the ladder is the country-neutral part.** Stated once, here, for every country module:

1. **The register publishes the date.** Quoted verbatim (`published_deadlines`, D-016(a)/D-018). Britain.
2. **The register publishes the entity's own period; we apply a cited statute to it.** D-016(b) with a *published* input. Britain's fallback rungs, and Sweden's new rung once (b) lands.
3. **The register publishes neither; we apply a cited statute to an assumed input.** Sweden today, Norway today. The assumption must be disclosed where it bites (D-023(a),(b)).
4. **Nothing.** No sourced rule, or an unclassified form (D-009(a)).

Rung 2 and rung 3 are different epistemic objects and `applies_because` must say which, in the sentence, not by omission. Sweden's rung-2 text names the observed year end, the period it was observed from, and the date that filing was registered — *"computed from the financial year end Bolagsverket's document list shows for this company's last filed annual report (period ending 2022-12-31, registered 2023-06-27)"* — so an agent can see both the fact and its age.

What does **not** change, ruled explicitly so nobody "fixes" it: `statutory_date == due_date` and `rolled_forward is False` on both Swedish deadlines. D-022(b) makes roll-forward a per-deadline fact decided by that deadline's own source, and `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §5.3 found no Swedish source either way. Knowing the year end tells us *which day* the rule lands on; it says nothing about whether that day moves. `period_end` becomes the real financial year end and `period_label` becomes its ISO date rather than a bare year — a broken year is not "2025" — while `period_start` stays `None` on rung 2, because pairing a known end with a start derived by subtracting twelve months would assert a period length the register never stated and bokföringslagen 3 kap. 3 § permits an 18-month first or final period. On rung 3 nothing moves at all: 1 January–31 December is one coherent assumption that N9 already discloses.

**One `VERIFY` gates rung 2 and only rung 2.** *"Inom sex månader från utgången av varje räkenskapsår"* (ABL 7 kap. 10 §) and *"inom sju månader från räkenskapsårets utgång"* (ÅRL 8 kap. 6 §) are ambiguous in one respect the calendar year hides: for a 30 April year end, is the AGM date 30 October (same day of month) or 31 October (end of the sixth month)? Both readings give 30 June and 31 July for 31 December, which is why nothing in the shipped build depends on the answer. The implementer must read **Bolagsverket's own published table of *sista dag för årsstämma* and *sista dag att lämna in årsredovisningen* by räkenskapsårsslut** and cite it; until it is cited, no non-December date is computed and rung 2 carries only the corrected prose. D-009: never guess a duty.

**And the invariant that makes this safe to ship: not one date moves for a 31 December company.** The observed year end reproduces the assumption exactly, so every existing SE deadline test stays green unchanged; the only diff on a December entity is `applies_because` and `notes` prose. That is the done-check, not a hope.

**(f) N9 does not disappear; it splits into three states, and it loses only the half that was false.** Block absent → today's text, with the false clause corrected and **one sentence naming the call that removes the assumption** (`include=["filings"]`), which is D-007's "a hint names the next call" applied to a note: a note that discloses an assumption without naming the call that would end it is half a note. Block present, year end 31 December → not an assumption at all, a **confirmation**: the sentence names the observed period end and its filing date. Block present, year end not 31 December → the same confirmation, plus the caveat that actually applies — a company may relay its räkenskapsår under bokföringslagen 3 kap., and only the period *end* is published, so a shortened or extended period is invisible to us. **Two clauses of N9 survive every state** and must not be dropped with the rest: ÅRL 8 kap. 3 §'s one-month-after-the-AGM rule (so seven months remains an outer limit even with a known year end) and 8 kap. 6 §'s nine-month variant. Neither has anything to do with the calendar year.

**(g) Filing history is in scope; the zip is not.** Ruled in, for three reasons that are not "we have the data": `CompanyReport.last_annual_accounts_year` already exists, is `None`, and `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` line ~490 marks it `DEFERRED` pointing at this exact endpoint, so ruling the block without settling it leaves a field permanently null beside the data that fills it (and (c) settles it the other way, which is the non-obvious answer); the list is already on the wire, so discarding `registreringstidpunkt` would be a second decision to reopen later; and for this product's actual use case — checking a counterparty before contracting — *"filed 2023-06-27 for the period ending 2022-12-31, 34 days before the late-fee point"* is worth more than a projected date, because it is a fact about behaviour rather than a projection. `documents` is sorted by `period_end` descending, then `filed_at` descending.

**Out: `GET /dokument/{dokumentId}`.** It returns `application/zip` — a third request per document, an XBRL package to parse, and a financial-statements feature wearing a filing-history costume. The block carries the *list*, never the document. `document_id` is carried anyway, as an opaque handle and the only key a Bolagsverket support case can name, documented as not fetchable through this API — `PublishedDeadline.source` is the precedent for carrying opaque upstream provenance we do not interpret.

*Declined: gating the second call on legal form, to keep a sole trader's personnummer off the wire.* It was considered because D-039 and D-040 make Swedish identifiers a live concern, and it is wrong twice: the identifier is already in the `POST /organisationer` body on the same TLS session to the same host, so a second POST body adds no exposure category that D-039 was about (URLs, access logs, `Referer`); and the gate would need a sourced list of which forms file an årsredovisning with Bolagsverket, which nobody has read. The fetch is unconditional when asked for. **Nothing in this entry adds a logging sink** — the block is fetched by identifier and the identifier never reaches `record_call` (D-040(b)) or a log line (D-040 addendum (ii)); the implementer must keep it that way in the new code path, including the second call's `X-Request-Id` DEBUG line, which logs the request id and never the body.

**(h) Three portal findings, ruled so a later reader does not code to the wrong thing.**

*`/dokumentlista` has no `dataproducent`/`fel` wrapper.* `DokumentlistaSvar` is `{"dokument": [ … ]}` — a plain array of plain objects. `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §1.6's "every field is a wrapper, therefore HTTP 200 ≠ data arrived" is an **`/organisationer` rule** and must be scoped to it; on `/dokumentlista` a 200 means the answer arrived, and the only failure signals are the HTTP status and the RFC 7807 body. Pointing the existing `fel`-inspecting helpers at this response would find nothing and mean nothing.

*No 404, and an empty list is never `not_found`.* `/dokumentlista` declares 200/400/401/403/500 only, with no `ORGANISATION_FINNS_EJ` equivalent — and `dokument` is **not** in the schema's `required` list, so the key may be absent as well as empty. Both map to the same thing: a present block with `documents: []`. **`/organisationer` decides whether an entity exists; `/dokumentlista` never does** — its behaviour on an unknown identifier is undocumented and unverified, so an empty document list must not be allowed to turn a successful lookup into a 404, and a filings failure must never fail the lookup (the block goes absent and a `notes` sentence says why, per (c)).

*The 400 example is a documentation bug on Bolagsverket's side.* All three data operations reference the same `ApiError-felbegaran` example, whose `detail` is *"Identitetsbeteckning har ogiltig kontrollsiffra."* — a check-digit complaint about an identifier that `GET /dokument/{dokumentId}` does not take, its only input being a document id we generated ourselves. **Never code to the `detail` string.** By operation: a 400 on `/organisationer` or `/dokumentlista` is `invalid_id` (the identifier is the only input, and D-032 already refuses to enforce a check digit we cannot source, so upstream's verdict decides); a 400 on `/dokument/{dokumentId}` is `upstream_error`, never `invalid_id`. Also note that every operation carries `x-throttling-tier: "Unlimited"` — a WSO2 gateway artefact that does not repeal the published 60/min (§1.5), and the second call spends a bucket token like any other.

The block gets its **own cache key and its own TTL** — `SE:bolagsverket:filings:{env}:{id}`, following the existing `_cache_key` convention, and the third user of the per-kind TTL table D-026(c) and D-028(2) both need (`core/cache.py:84-94`), built once by whichever lands first. **24 h for a non-empty list, 1 h for an empty one.** That is D-006's positive/negative asymmetry reused for D-006's own reason: an empty list is the answer that goes stale the instant the company files, and during filing season a six-day-old "nothing filed" about a company that filed on Tuesday is wrong in the direction that harms a counterparty check. *Declined: D-026(c)'s 7-day GLEIF TTL.* An LEI renews annually on no particular day; an annual report lands in a three-month window everyone can predict.

**(i) Sequencing: this is `R-5b`, between R-5 and R-6, and it does not take a new roadmap number.** It cannot precede R-5 — `SourceRef` and `include=[…]` do not exist in `src/` today, and hand-rolling them here would build the mechanism twice, which is the one thing R-5 exists to prevent. It should not be folded *into* R-5 either: R-5's value is getting the mechanism right on a single lowest-risk payload, and two payloads in one task is how a mechanism acquires a payload-shaped bend. It goes **before R-6** because it corrects a wrong date we are shipping today, which outranks a capability we do not ship at all; because it is the cheapest possible proof that R-5's mechanism survives a second, differently-shaped upstream — specifically the *same-register* case, which is the one that stresses (c)'s rule and which R-6 (a different organisation, a different licence) does not test; and because R-6 is still blocked on unverified SMP endpoints (`CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §2) while this one is blocked on nothing but R-5. It costs about a day, so it does not threaten R-6's 1 January 2027 clock. It takes `R-5b` rather than a new number because renumbering R-6…R-8 would falsify every "Applies to tasks" line in this file that cites them, and because it is a *user* of R-5's mechanism rather than a mechanism of its own. **(a)'s prose correction is not sequenced at all** — it is six strings, no `core/`, no shape, and it should land in the next task that touches Sweden.
Reason: the deadline feature is the differentiator, and D-016 settled that the way to keep it trustworthy across countries is a rule about *provenance* rather than about arithmetic. This is that rule meeting its hardest case so far, and it turns out to sharpen two earlier entries rather than merely applying them. D-026(c) justified attachments by *whose data it is*, which was the wrong reason for the right shape; the reason that survives Sweden is *how many fetches it took*, and stating it that way is what stops the next architect from putting a second-round-trip value onto a first-round-trip field because the type happened to fit. And D-023(c) already ruled, for Norway, that a gap we can close must not be described as a boundary we cannot — Sweden made the same mistake four months later in the same file, in prose an agent reads before it calls anything, which is the argument for writing the ladder in (e) down once instead of rediscovering it per country.
Applies to tasks: T31 (this entry, `tasks/T31.md`), R-5 (the `include` argument must be designed to extend to `company_deadlines`), a new R-5b, R-1 (`include` is refused on the batch path), T26-family follow-up for (a)'s six strings, and D-023(d) for Norway, which is unblocked by this shape

### D-042 — Depth per country: a second call to any register is an `include=[…]` attachment, R-5 is promoted to first and is the gate, D-024's tool *count* is replaced by a tool *rule*, and officers and PSC stay barred
Date: 2026-09-08
Decision: Kim's 2026-09-08 instruction — *"more registries on each folder would be MUCH better"* — and the overnight measurement behind it (`~/mcp-growth/USE-CASE.md` §2 Evidence 3, `COMPETITORS.md` §8.2, `ADOPTION.md`, all read before writing this). `bouch/uk-due-diligence`: **one** country, six registers, sixteen tools, no key, **60,787** Smithery uses. `sophymarine/openregistry`: **thirty** countries, key required, **3**. `apier/apier`: Norway only, key, **84**. Ours: three countries, seven tools, no key, **0**. Against that, our own depth is one Companies House endpoint out of nine on a key we already hold, `/enheter` + `/underenheter` for Norway, and two Swedish endpoints marked `DEFERRED`. This entry rules the shape of the correction. It is ten parts and it amends three earlier entries by name.

**(a) What the numbers support, and what they do not — because the wrong reading of them would cost a tool surface permanently.** The signal that survives inspection is **depth per jurisdiction**: one-country servers hold five of the top six rows (60,787 / 2,235 / 1,596 / 1,150 / 84) and the two broadest are at the bottom (thirty countries → 3; "global" → 314). What is **not** supported is that *tool count* is the mechanism. On the same table `sophymarine/openregistry` ships ten tools for 3 uses while `vdineshk/sg-company-lookup-mcp` ships four for 2,235, and `entia/entity-verification` ships twelve across ten countries for 16,045 — a rank correlation with tool count that inverts twice. Sixteen tools is what *bouch* happens to look like after covering six separate registers with six unrelated payload shapes; it is not the reason anyone connected. **So the strategy changes and the tool surface does not, and (c) is where that is argued rather than asserted.** One further caution recorded so it is not rediscovered: `useCount` measures Smithery-proxied calls and structurally favours keyless remote servers, `~/mcp-growth/USE-CASE.md` §6 test 1 is the falsifier, and this entry's rulings are chosen to be right whether or not that test passes — because every one of them is also a correctness or provenance argument that stands alone.

Also recorded, and **not ruled here because it is not a shape question**: *bouch* is keyless and we are not, for GB and SE. D-017 and D-030 already draw that line (the hosted service holds the keys; a `uvx` user supplies their own), and it belongs to the adoption track, not to this file.

**(b) The shape: every additional register call is an attachment under `include=[…]`, and R-5 is a hard gate.** D-041(c) settled the discipline in the sharpest available words — ***one fetch, one `SourceRef`* — not one organisation** — and gave the three reasons a plain field cannot do the job: two round trips have two moments, two independent failure modes and two independent cache states. Every item in this entry is a second round trip to a register, so every item is an attachment; there is no version of depth that is a set of new fields on `CompanyReport`.

That makes `SourceRef` and `include=[…]` — **R-5 in `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §7, which does not exist in `src/` today** (`grep -rn "SourceRef" src/` is empty) — a precondition, not a nice-to-have. **Nothing in this entry may be implemented before R-5 lands.**

*Declined: a cheaper shape that skips R-5.* Three were considered and all three fail on D-041(c) rather than on effort. **Plain nullable fields on `CompanyReport`** (`charges_outstanding: int | None`) put a second-round-trip value under a `fetched_at` that never applied to it and cannot say *this half failed*; that is the exact defect D-041(b),(c) exist to prevent, and it is worse here than it was in Sweden because a charge is a credit decision. **A hand-rolled per-block provenance dict** builds R-5's mechanism a second time in a country module and guarantees two shapes for one concept — the single thing R-5 exists to prevent (D-041(i)). **MCP resources** (`registry://charges/GB/00445790`) genuinely avoid the tool budget, and are declined because a resource is pull-by-URI, most clients never enumerate them for a model, and the block would still need a provenance shape — so it costs R-5 anyway and buys unreachability. The honest statement is: **R-5 is about a day and a half of work that is blocked on nothing** (`CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §2: "Not blocked, start when the freeze lifts: R-1, R-3, R-4, R-5"), and the correct response to a gate that cheap is to walk through it, not around it.

**One requirement is added to R-5 by this entry, and it is the reason the gate is worth waiting for.** R-5 must put the assembly in **`core/registry.py`**, not in the two surfaces: a concrete `async def lookup_with(self, id: str, include: Sequence[str]) -> CompanyReport` that calls `lookup`, then each requested attachment method with D-024(g)'s bounded concurrency, attaches what succeeded, and — in **one** place, once — leaves a failed attachment's block **absent** and appends the `notes` sentence saying so. Written per surface it drifts within a release; written per country it is copied three times; D-001 forbids the first and D-018's whole finding is the second. Each attachment is a **concrete** method on `Registry` named exactly as its `include` value, raising `not_implemented` by default (D-024's `lookup_many` and D-028(5)'s `officers` are the precedent), so the ABC stays four abstract methods wide and `registries/xx/` is never edited.

**(c) The tool budget: D-024's *count* is amended into a *rule*, and the rule authorises zero new tools today.** D-024 paragraph one ratified "five MCP tools", amended once by D-031 to "five registry tools plus two connector aliases". Kim is right that this is a decision and not a law, and the usage data has falsified the *reason* the count was defended — but (a) shows it has not falsified the count itself. So the count is replaced by the thing it was always standing in for:

> **A tool is bought by a distinct question an agent asks. It is never bought by an endpoint we call, by a register we add, or by a country we support.** The ceiling remains the ~40-tool retrieval budget (`client-support-discovery-and-tool-limits.md`); the binding constraint below that ceiling is that **every tool name must be a distinct retrieval key**, because the failure mode of a near-duplicate is the agent calling the *wrong* tool (D-024(a), D-031(a)).

D-024 paragraph one, as amended by D-031(a), therefore reads: *"`registry-mcp` exposes five registry tools plus two connector aliases. A further tool requires an explicit amendment of this entry, in this file, satisfying D-042(c)'s rule — the distinct question argued rather than assumed."* **The published count does not move**, so D-031(f)'s four wording locations and `README.md`'s "Seven tools, not fifty" are correct as they stand and are not touched by this entry.

Applying it: "does this company have charges against it", "is it filing on time", "did it exit administration" are all the same question — *tell me about this company* — asked at different depths, by a caller who has already chosen `lookup_company`. They buy no tool. **Zero new registry tools are authorised by this entry.** Note also what the rule kills: the argument that depth does not scale to more countries. Our tools take `country`, so an attachment is one `include` value across every country that declares it — the surface grows with *blocks*, not with countries × blocks, and *bouch* has sixteen tools only because it has six unrelated payload shapes in one country and no country argument at all.

*Declined: a tool per endpoint (the sixteen-tool shape).* It is the winner's shape and it is still wrong for us, for a reason that is ours and not general: with `country` on every tool, `company_charges` would be one tool that raises `not_implemented` for two of three countries — a retrieval key that is a dead end two thirds of the time, which is a worse advertisement than not shipping it.
*Declined: one tool with a discriminated `section` argument* (`get_company_detail(id, country, section)`). It is `include=[…]` with a second name and a second code path, so it fails D-004's one-shape rule: two operations returning the same block, one of which cannot also return the company record, and an agent choosing between them for no reason.
*Declined: grouped tools now* (one `company_background` over all blocks). Same block, second placement, before any evidence that the first placement fails.
*Declined: REST sub-routes per block* (`GET /v1/{country}/company/{id}/charges`). D-041(b) declined exactly this and its reason holds: D-028(5) gave officers a sub-route because that block has a **privacy story of its own** a caller may want to fetch alone, which is not true of a charge. `?include=` on the existing company route, and nothing else. Revisit only if a caller asks.

**The escape hatch, named in advance so it is chosen by evidence and not by taste** — the D-031(a) pattern. The real cost of `include=[…]` is not budget, it is **discoverability**: an enum value inside an input schema is not a retrieval key. (d) pays that cost without tools. If, after (d) has shipped, the eval shows agents failing to reach a block they were asked for, the fix is **one** grouped tool returning the identical blocks from the identical builder, whose **name is taken from the eval's own failing prompts**, and D-024 paragraph one is amended again in the same release. It is not authorised now.

**(d) Discoverability is paid in three places, all free.** *(1)* `Registry` gains `supported_includes: ClassVar[frozenset[str]] = frozenset()`; `CountryInfo` gains `supported_includes: list[str]` (sorted, for a stable wire), filled by `country_info()`. So `list_countries` — a tool an agent already calls — answers "what more can you tell me about a GB company" in one round trip, with no upstream request. *(2)* **The available block names appear in `lookup_company`'s and `company_deadlines`' description text, not only in the schema's enum**, because under Claude Code's tool search the name and first sentence are the retrieval key; the words *charges*, *filing history*, *insolvency* and *annual accounts* must reach that string or they reach no index anywhere. *(3)* D-026(c)'s rule is sharpened: an `include` value that the **resolved country** does not declare is `bad_request` whose `hint` names *that country's* allowed set — **never silently ignored, and never an empty block**. That last clause is D-011's invariant and is the load-bearing half: an empty `ChargeBlock` must mean "this register publishes charges and this company has none", and if an undeclared country could also return one it would mean "we cannot see charges here" as well, which is the two-states-into-one collapse this file has now refused four times (D-011, D-026(b), D-028(3), D-041(d)).

**(e) What is in scope: the test is field-level, not endpoint-level — and Kim's assumed first tranche fails it in three places.** The brief proposed charges, filing history, insolvency and registers as "company data rather than personal data". Tested against the payloads rather than the endpoint names, that is wrong three times, and the third case is the dangerous one.

The test, stated once for every country module: **a payload is person-bearing if any field in its schema can carry a natural person's name or any other particular of one.** By endpoint:

* **GB `/company/{n}/charges`** — `persons_entitled[].name`. Usually a bank; sometimes a director lending to their own company. A bare name, no date of birth, no address, no nationality.
* **GB `/company/{n}/insolvency`** — `cases[].practitioners[]` carries a licensed individual's **name and address**. The most person-bearing of the four.
* **GB `/company/{n}/filing-history`** — `items[].description_values` resolves description templates and carries `officer_name` (and, on `legacy` items, free prose). **Relayed whole, filing history is an officers feed wearing a filing-history costume**: appointments, terminations and names, which is precisely what D-028 bars, formatted as something else. This is the finding this part exists to record.
* **GB `/company/{n}/registers`, `/exemptions`, `/uk-establishments`** — clean. Register-location metadata, PSC-exemption periods, and establishment numbers.
* **NO `data.brreg.no/regnskapsregisteret/regnskap/{orgnr}`** (open part) — clean. Key figures and `regnskapsperiode`. Free, keyless, verified live (`NORBIZ_SPEC.md` line ~278), labelled *preview, no guarantees of quality of service*.
* **SE `POST /dokumentlista`** — clean. Already ruled by D-041.

**Three minimisation rules make the tranche clean, and each costs the use case nothing.**

1. **`description_values` is read through an allow-list of keys, never wholesale, and the allow-list in this entry is exactly one key: `made_up_date`.** A key is added to it by an entry in this file, never by an implementer. `items[].description` — which is the register's own *template key*, e.g. `appoint-person-director-company-with-name-date`, not prose — is relayed **verbatim** as `description_code`. The template key already says *a director was appointed*; only the values say *who*. That is minimisation using a field the register itself publishes, not a redaction we invent, and it is greppable: `grep -n "description_values" src/registry_mcp/registries/gb/` must show exactly one key.
2. **Insolvency practitioners are not relayed at all in the first tranche.** Case type, case number and the register's own dated events carry the entire distress signal for a pre-contract check; "who to send the claim to" is a different job for a different caller. Adding practitioners later is a decision with its own entry, and it inherits D-028's preconditions in full because a name plus an address is precisely what D-028 is about.
3. **`persons_entitled` is relayed, renamed `parties_entitled: list[str]`, and bound by D-028(1).** It is a term of a company's own instrument — the charge is the company's encumbrance and the chargee is a term of it — it carries no other particular, and the credit question is unanswerable without it. The rename is not cosmetic: "persons" asserts a natural person, which the register does not. **D-028(1) binds here and is stated as a rule, not an aspiration**: no name in this list is ever a lookup key, ever an index, ever searchable, and never reaches a log (D-040). `legal/terms.md` gains one sentence naming this as the one place a natural person's name can reach a response; the R-8 legal review is asked about it in the same breath, and until that review this list is the **only** person-bearing field the product ships.

**(f) What is barred, said plainly so nobody reads "depth" as permission.** **Officers and PSC are out.** GB `/officers`, GB `/persons-with-significant-control`, NO `/enheter/{orgnr}/roller`, and every equivalent in every future country. D-028's four preconditions are unchanged and none is met: a lawyer-reviewed lawful basis does not exist, the person-record TTL does not exist, `suppressed` cannot yet be derived, and the ENK/enskild notes are the only part in flight. Also barred by the same reasoning and named individually because each has a plausible-sounding case: **any person→company path** (D-028(1)); **`description_values.officer_name`** and the `legacy` free-text description, by (e)(1); **insolvency practitioners**, by (e)(2); and **beneficial ownership anywhere**, GB PSC included, notwithstanding that it is free and structured. Depth is authorised over *company facts*. It is not a re-opening of D-028, and the sentence in `legal/terms.md` — *"we do not create profiles, enrich, or combine this data with any other source"* — must still be true after every task this entry authorises.

**(g) The country-neutral premise survives, but it binds `CompanyReport` and not an attachment — and that is the honest answer, not a save.** `CompanyReport` stays one shape across countries: that promise is kept, is still worth keeping, and is what makes `lookup_company` a single tool. An **attachment is a different object**: it is opt-in, it is declared per country, it carries its own provenance, and a caller who wrote `include=["charges"]` has already named a country and already knows they asked a country-specific question. So:

* **Every attachment model lives in `core/models.py` and its field names are country-neutral** — D-004 unamended, national vocabulary in *values* only. **No country gets a private payload model.** *Declined: country-scoped payloads under the attachment* (`charges: GBChargeBlock | NOChargeBlock`). It would make the attachment's shape depend on the country at the type level, fork every client's parsing, and put country knowledge back into `core/` by way of a union — D-001 and D-004 both, and the loss is not theoretical: the moment Norway's Løsøreregisteret opens, a shared `Charge` gets filled and a scoped one gets a migration.
* **An attachment declared by exactly one country is expected and fine.** It is the asymmetry `vat_registered`, `employees` and `share_capital` already have on `CompanyReport`, moved up one level.
* **The anti-bend rule.** A field is added to a shared attachment model when *some* register publishes it and its name is neutral; a field only one register can plausibly ever fill must say so and say why in its own description. D-041's `days_from_fee_point` is the worked precedent and it stands.
* **The widest payload defines a shared model; the narrowest fills a subset.** This is why (i) re-sequences GB ahead of Sweden.

Applied to the three facts the brief asked about: **a filing generalises** — Britain, Norway (Regnskapsregisteret's `regnskapsperiode` + registration date) and Sweden (`rapporteringsperiodTom` + `registreringstidpunkt`) all publish one, so `FilingHistory` is shared and its three fillers are the proof. **A charge does not, yet** — Norway's Løsøreregisteret offers only "a limited, standardized machine-to-machine service … for external partners" and Sweden's företagsinteckningar are not in the free API, so `ChargeBlock` is a shared model with one declaring country and two named future fillers. **An insolvency *case* does not either** — the insolvency *status* is already country-neutral and already shipped (`CompanyStatus`, `konkurs`, `registers["insolvency"]`), and only the case history is GB-shaped. The premise that gives way is not "one shape"; it is **"every country answers every question"**, which was never written down and was never true — `search_company` is already `not_implemented` for Sweden.

**(h) `FiledDocument` is widened by three fields, now, and Britain defines the shape before Sweden fills it.** D-041(d)'s model was designed against one payload — annual reports with a reporting period — and it is neutral in its names but Swedish in its field list. Britain's filing history is any filing, so it needs, and every other register will need:

```python
    category: str | None = None          # the register's own category, verbatim: "accounts", "mortgage", "gazette"
    type_code: str | None = None         # the register's own form code, verbatim: "AA", "CS01", "MR01"
    description_code: str | None = None  # the register's own description template key, verbatim, never resolved (e)(1)
```

Nothing is removed. `kind` keeps D-041's meaning — the `Deadline` slug this filing discharges, `None` when it discharges none — so GB maps `category == "accounts"` → `"annual_accounts"` and `category == "confirmation-statement"` → `"confirmation_statement"`, and the other twenty-odd categories map to `None` rather than to an invented slug (D-009). `days_from_fee_point` is **`None` for GB**, because Companies House publishes `accounts.next_due` for the *next* period and no per-period historical due date, so the datum the field is measured against does not exist — stated in `notes`, not left to inference. `period_start` stays `None` for GB and SE and is filled by NO.

**(i) Sequencing, and it does not renumber anything** — D-041(i)'s reasoning, reused: renumbering falsifies every "Applies to tasks" line in this file. **R-5 is promoted from fifth to first.** It was ranked fifth when it was machinery two future items needed; it is now the machinery the whole product direction rests on, it is blocked on nothing, and it costs about a day and a half. New items take letters under R-5, as `R-5b` already does:

| | Item | Task | Why here |
|---|---|---|---|
| **R-5** | `SourceRef`, `include=[…]`, `lookup_with`, per-kind TTL, LEI | — | The gate. Payload stays the LEI: CC0, keyless, no personal data, no clock, already spec'd and done-checked (`CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §7). Plus (b)'s `lookup_with` and (d)'s `supported_includes` |
| **R-5c** | **GB `include=["filings", "charges"]`** | **T37** | Cheapest depth we own: key already held, deadlines already at rung 1 so no ladder work, no personal-identifier logging concern, and the **widest** filing payload, which by (g) is the one that must define the shared model. Also the first test of **two** `include` values in one call, which R-5's own done-check never exercises |
| **R-5b** | SE `include=["filings"]` | T31b | Unchanged in content; **moves behind R-5c** so it fills a model rather than defining one (see below) |
| **R-5d** | NO `include=["filings"]` from Regnskapsregisteret's open part | new | Third filler, no new model, closes D-023(d) and moves Norway's annual deadlines from rung 3 to rung 2 — the same correction D-041 made for Sweden, in the country where we have the most users |
| **R-5e** | GB `include=["insolvency"]` | new | Small, and (e)(2) has already ruled the only hard part |

**This amends D-041(i) on one point and only one.** D-041(i) placed R-5b immediately after R-5, before R-6, and every reason it gave still holds — it corrects a wrong date we are shipping today, it is the cheapest proof R-5's mechanism survives a same-register second fetch, and it is blocked on nothing else. It moves behind R-5c because a fact D-041 could not have weighed had not yet arrived: **Britain's filing payload is a superset of Sweden's, and (g) rules that the superset defines the model.** Building Sweden first means designing `FiledDocument` against annual reports and then widening it under Britain — the payload-shaped bend D-041(i) itself warned about, arriving one task later than it feared. The delay is one task and R-6's 1 January 2027 clock is untouched. **D-041(a)'s prose correction is still not sequenced at all** and should land in the next task that touches Sweden.

**R-2 keeps its slot regardless.** It is two nullable fields, it blocks nothing, and Erhvervsstyrelsen's answer is due ~2026-09-23; it may be taken by anyone free at any point in this order. R-1, R-3 and R-4 slide behind the depth track — with 0 uses, a meter measures nothing and a batch path serves nobody. **`include` remains refused on the batch path** (D-041(b)), unchanged and now for a second reason: two attachments × fifty rows is 150 upstream requests.

**(j) Mechanics that would otherwise be re-decided per block, ruled once.**

* **Cache.** One key per attachment on the existing convention — `{COUNTRY}:{registry}:{name}:{id}` — and its own TTL from the per-kind table (`core/cache.py:84-94`) that D-026(c), D-028(2) and D-041(h) all need and none has built. **24 h non-empty, 1 h empty**, D-006's asymmetry for D-006's reason: an empty result is the one that goes stale the instant something is filed or a charge is registered, and a stale "no charges" is wrong in the direction that harms a credit decision.
* **Truncation is disclosed, never silent.** One page per attachment, never paginated — an unbounded history is an unbounded context cost and a multiplied upstream cost. The block carries the register's own `total_count`, and when it exceeds what we returned, a `notes` sentence says so and names the register's own URL. Silently returning 25 of 300 filings is the quiet inaccuracy this project exists to avoid. Page sizes differ per block and the reasons differ: **filings 25**, newest first, which is five to ten years of accounts and confirmation statements; **charges the register's maximum**, because a charge's relevance does not decay with age and an old outstanding charge is the whole answer.
* **A derived neutral flag never guesses.** Where a block needs a country-neutral yes/no over a register's own vocabulary — `Charge.is_outstanding` over `status` — it is derived by **membership of a committed table**, and a status word the table does not contain yields `None`, never `False`. D-025(d)'s rule, and D-011's.
* **A failed attachment never fails the lookup**, the block is absent, and a `notes` sentence says which fetch failed. D-041(c),(h), now implemented once in `lookup_with` per (b).
* **A new source is not a new decision.** A further register that publishes a *company* fact is a new `include` value under these rules and needs no entry here. A register that publishes a *person* fact needs D-028 satisfied first, whatever it is called. `~/mcp-growth/DEPTH.md`, being written in parallel, ranks the candidates; it changes nothing in this entry.

Reason: the organising idea this project was built on — one JSON shape across many countries — turns out to be a property people like once they are using the server and not a reason anybody picks it, and the measured version of that is four orders of magnitude wide. The correction is real and Kim is right to make it. What this entry spends its length on is the two ways the correction could be implemented into a permanent mistake: buying depth with tool slots, which is the winner's shape and is wrong for a server that already takes a `country` argument; and buying it with second-round-trip values on first-round-trip fields, which D-041 had just finished ruling out and which is far more tempting when the payload is a charge than when it was a Swedish year end. The pleasant surprise is that neither the country-neutral model nor the five-tool restraint has to be sacrificed to get there — what has to go is the unwritten assumption that every country answers every question, and that one was never true. The unpleasant one is in (e): the tranche that looked cleanest, filing history, is the one that quietly relays officer names, and it would have shipped that way.
Applies to tasks: R-5 (promoted to first; gains `lookup_with` and `supported_includes`), **R-5c / `tasks/T37.md`** (this entry), R-5b / T31b (unchanged in content, re-sequenced behind R-5c), new R-5d (Norway) and R-5e (GB insolvency), `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §1 (order) and §7 (R-5's scope); amends D-024 paragraph one (count → rule), D-041(d) (`FiledDocument` widened by three fields) and D-041(i) (R-5b re-sequenced behind R-5c); **D-028 is not amended, D-031 is not amended, and the published tool count does not move**; D-023(d) is unblocked by R-5d

### D-043 — The Norwegian key figures are a second block, not a wider `FiledDocument`: `include=["financials"]`, one fetch serving two blocks, and a figure that may never be separated from its currency
Date: 2026-09-08
Decision: `registries/no/accounts.py` (R-5d, behind the seam) left ~20 numeric fields on the wire, unclaimed, and escalated rather than widening a model three country modules are converging on. It was right to stop, and it was right for a reason it only half-stated. Eleven parts. This entry amends D-042(g) and D-042(j) by name, and sharpens D-041(c); it amends neither D-028 nor D-024, and the published tool count does not move.

Everything asserted below about the payload was verified live and keyless on 2026-09-08 against `https://data.brreg.no/regnskapsregisteret/regnskap/{orgnr}`: **573 successful responses** — 358 from a 600-organisation sample spread across `AS`, `ASA`, `SA`, `BRL`, `STI`, `ANS`, `DA` and `NUF` (236 of that 600 answered 404, 6 answered something else), and 215 more from entities the Enhetsregisteret marks `underAvvikling`. Where a number appears below it is a count out of one of those two populations, not an impression.

**(a) The implementer's own argument, tested — half of it holds and the half it leaned on does not.** Its stated reason was D-041(g): key figures are *"a financial-statements feature wearing a filing-history costume"*, the sentence that ruled Bolagsverket's zip out. That reason is **not sufficient**, and it must not be reused as though it were. D-041(g) ruled out the zip because it was *a third request, an XBRL package to parse, and bytes we would have to interpret* — none of which is true here. These figures arrive parsed, in the same JSON body, on the same request R-5d already makes, at zero marginal upstream cost. If "financial statements" were by itself a bar, this entry would end here and it should not: the bar D-041(g) actually set was *parsing filed documents*, and this clears it.

The reason that survives is the second one, which the module raised only procedurally — *a unilateral widening would break the "wiring is a rename" property* — and it turns out to be a correctness argument, not a courtesy. It is argued in (b).

Two corrections to that module's own docstring, both from the sweep, so a later reader does not trust a claim the wire disproves. **The payload's key set is not closed at the leaves.** `resultatregnskapResultat.totalresultat` is present on 149 of 358 responses and absent on the other 209 (Equinor `923609016` is one of the absentees); `regnkapsprinsipper.regnskapsregler` has **three** values, not two — `regnskapslovenAlminneligRegler` (338), `IFRS` (12), `forenkletAnvendelseIFRS` (8). The *top-level* key set is closed, and the minimisation finding in that docstring stands untouched: no field in this payload can carry a natural person's name or any particular of one, and this block ships none. D-042(e) is satisfied without a redaction, exactly as recorded.

**(b) It is a second block, and the deciding reason is D-011's invariant rather than taste.** *Declined: widening `FiledDocument` with the figures.* D-042(g)'s anti-bend rule does not forbid a one-country field — it requires such a field to say so in its own description, and `days_from_fee_point` is the worked precedent — so the anti-bend rule alone would permit this. Four things defeat it anyway, and the fourth is decisive:

1. **Scale.** One documented one-country field is a footnote; nineteen of them are a change of subject. `FiledDocument` today answers *when did they file, for what period, what kind of document*. A balance sheet on it makes filing history wear a financial-statements costume — D-041(g)'s own category error, mirrored.
2. **Context cost.** The block is the unit a caller pays for. An agent checking punctuality does not want a balance sheet in its window; an agent checking solvency does not want `journalnr`.
3. **D-042(c)'s rule, applied one level down.** *A tool is bought by a distinct question an agent asks.* The same test decides a block: **"is it filing on time" and "what do the numbers say" are two questions**, and `~/registry-mcp/research/07-product-improvements.md` item 14 names the second one in the caller's own words — *"is this supplier solvent?"* — as an independently ranked, revenue-relevant gap. That is what buys a distinct thing.
4. **The nullability collapse, which is the one that settles it.** Britain declares `filings` and fills `FiledDocument` richly. Put the figures on that model and every GB `FiledDocument` carries nineteen `None` financial fields, where `None` means *Britain does not publish this*; on a Norwegian one the same `None` means *the company did not report this line* — and (f) shows that second state is real, common and load-bearing. **Two states in one field.** This file has refused that collapse five times (D-011, D-026(b), D-028(3), D-041(d), D-042(d)(3)) and refuses it a sixth. A separate block that Britain simply does not declare keeps them apart: **an absent block means the country does not publish figures; a `None` field inside a present block means the company did not report that line.**

*Declined: `include=["accounts"]` as the name.* "Accounts" is Companies House's own word for the filed document (`category == "accounts"`, `accounts.next_due`), so a GB caller reading `accounts` in our `include` enum would reasonably expect the filing and get a `bad_request` — a retrieval key that means two things is the failure D-024(a) and D-042(c) are both about. *Declined: `key_figures`.* It is brreg's *nøkkeltall* in English clothes: national vocabulary in a field name, which D-004 puts in values only. **The value is `financials`**, the field is `CompanyReport.financials`, and the words *financial figures*, *turnover*, *profit*, *balance sheet* and *solvency* must reach `lookup_company`'s description text per D-042(d)(2), not only the schema enum.

**(c) The shape.** `core/models.py` gains four models; `CompanyReport` gains one field, `financials: FinancialSummary | None = None`, `null` on every country that was not asked. Names are country-neutral (D-042(g)); national vocabulary lives in `scope` and `accounting_framework` **values** only.

```python
class IncomeStatement(_Base):        # flows over a period
    revenue: float | None = None                    # sumDriftsinntekter
    operating_costs: float | None = None            # sumDriftskostnad
    operating_result: float | None = None           # driftsresultat
    financial_income: float | None = None           # sumFinansinntekter
    financial_costs: float | None = None            # sumFinanskostnad
    net_financial_items: float | None = None        # nettoFinans
    profit_before_tax: float | None = None          # ordinaertResultatFoerSkattekostnad
    profit_for_period: float | None = None          # aarsresultat
    total_comprehensive_income: float | None = None # totalresultat

class BalanceSheet(_Base):           # stocks at the period's last instant
    fixed_assets: float | None = None               # sumAnleggsmidler
    current_assets: float | None = None             # sumOmloepsmidler
    total_assets: float | None = None               # sumEiendeler
    paid_in_equity: float | None = None             # sumInnskuttEgenkaptial (the register's own spelling)
    retained_equity: float | None = None            # sumOpptjentEgenkapital
    equity: float | None = None                     # sumEgenkapital
    non_current_liabilities: float | None = None    # sumLangsiktigGjeld
    current_liabilities: float | None = None        # sumKortsiktigGjeld
    liabilities: float | None = None                # sumGjeld
    total_equity_and_liabilities: float | None = None  # sumEgenkapitalGjeld

class FinancialPeriod(_Base):
    period_start: date | None = None
    period_end: date | None = None
    currency: str                    # REQUIRED, no default — see (d)
    accounting_framework: str | None = None   # regnskapsregler, verbatim
    scope: str | None = None                  # regnskapstype, verbatim
    consolidated: bool | None = None          # derived from `scope` by committed table
    small_entity: bool | None = None          # regnkapsprinsipper.smaaForetak
    audit_exempt: bool | None = None          # revisjon.fravalgRevisjon
    unaudited: bool | None = None             # revisjon.ikkeRevidertAarsregnskap, uninverted
    liquidation_basis: bool | None = None     # avviklingsregnskap
    document_id: str | None = None            # journalnr — the same handle as the sibling FiledDocument
    income_statement: IncomeStatement | None = None
    balance_sheet: BalanceSheet | None = None

class FinancialSummary(_Base):
    periods: list[FinancialPeriod] = []       # sorted by period_end descending
    provenance: SourceRef
    notes: list[str] = []
```

The nesting is not decoration and it is the one structural choice here that is not obvious. `CompanyReport` is flat because it is one register's record of one entity at one moment; a financial statement is **two objects with two different time semantics** — `revenue` is a flow over a span, `total_assets` is a stock at an instant — and a caller who mixes them makes precisely the error this block exists to prevent. Two named sub-objects make the difference visible in the JSON. Both are `None` when the register published no line in that statement at all.

*Declined: `figures: dict[str, float]`.* Untyped, unvalidatable under `extra="forbid"`, and it puts the register's own key names into the wire — D-004 in three ways at once. *Declined: `Decimal`.* `CompanyReport.share_capital` is already `float | None`, every value observed is well inside float64's exact-integer range, and the register's own precision is coarser than float error by six orders of magnitude (see (d)). *Declined: dropping the sparse fields* (`total_comprehensive_income` 149/358, `non_current_liabilities` 194/358). They are on the wire, they are totals, and discarding them is a second decision to reopen later — D-041(g)'s own reasoning. *Declined: carrying `oppstillingsplan`.* Only `"store"` was observed across all 573 responses, so the field would be a constant; what a caller wants from it — which lines exist — is answered by which fields are `None`, and the regime is carried by `small_entity`. If a second value ever appears, an entry adds the field. *Declined: carrying `virksomhet.morselskap` onto `CompanyReport.in_group`.* That is a second-round-trip value landing on a first-round-trip field, which is exactly what D-041(c) forbids, and `in_group`/`parent_id` are already filled from `/enheter`. *Declined: `total_count` on the block.* The register publishes no count and returns one period; a field that is permanently `None` asserts nothing. The one-period fact is a note.

**(d) Currency, scale and basis: the unit travels with the figure, or the figure does not travel — and the model makes separation unrepresentable rather than discouraged.**

**`currency` is a required field with no default on `FinancialPeriod`.** A period whose `valuta` the register omits is **not carried at all**, and a `notes` sentence says why. This is safe — `valuta` was present on 573 of 573 responses — and it is the point: under `extra="forbid"` plus a required field, a currency-less figure cannot be constructed. That is the difference between a rule and a warning.

It is not a theoretical trap. **12 of 358 Norwegian filings are not in kroner** — USD 7, EUR 2, SEK 2, DKK 1 — so an agent comparing two *Norwegian* companies can be wrong without ever crossing a border. Equinor ASA `923609016` files in USD.

**Scale is whole units of `currency`, and precision is not.** Equinor's `sumEiendeler` is `103432000000.0` — whole US dollars — but its `sumEgenkapitalGjeld` is `103431000000.0`, one million lower, because the company filed in millions and the register multiplied up. The same artefact appears at 10³ across the sample. So the values are exact integers of the reporting currency and are **not** significant to that precision; nothing may present them as though they were, and no field records the original scale because the register does not publish one.

**Basis travels too, and it varies inside one country.** `accounting_framework` carries `regnskapsregler` verbatim: `regnskapslovenAlminneligRegler` (338), `IFRS` (12), `forenkletAnvendelseIFRS` (8). Two Norwegian companies' `profit_for_period` are not necessarily on the same basis. *Declined: a derived `ifrs: bool`.* Three values do not fold into two without putting simplified-IFRS arbitrarily on one side — D-025(d) and D-011.

**No cross-country arithmetic is permitted, and none is possible through this model.** No figure is ever converted, normalised, restated, rebased or annualised. We hold no FX rate, and choosing one would require inventing a date the register never published — D-009 in the purest form the project has met. The block carries one unconditional `notes` sentence saying the figures are denominated in the stated currency, prepared under the stated framework, and are not comparable across companies or borders without regard to both.

**(e) No arithmetic produces a figure. Exactly one comparison is permitted, and its output is a caveat, not a number.** *Declined: `equity_ratio`, `current_ratio`, `net_debt`, `working_capital`, `is_solvent`, `solvency: str`, and every other derived indicator.* Three independent reasons, any one sufficient. **(1) The register's own totals do not reconcile.** `sumEiendeler != sumEgenkapitalGjeld` on **27 of 358** filings (7.5%) — mostly the ±1-unit rounding of (d), but not always: `931626469` reports total assets `0` against equity-and-liabilities `30,000`; `880998412` reports `221,199` against `9,000`; `927866951` reports `195,395,000` against `140,395,000`. And `sumGjeld` is **negative** on real filings — `931883836` at `−108,837`, `989149202` at `−11,205` — which is not a company fact but a filing the register relayed without validating. A ratio built here is a ratio of two numbers the register itself does not vouch for jointly. **(2)** By (f), an absent line is not a zero, so any sum or difference touching one is unknowable, and a `None`-propagating ratio is a field that is `None` for reasons a caller cannot tell apart — D-011 again. **(3)** A ratio is a verdict. D-041(d) already refused `filed_late: bool` in favour of a measurement against a named datum; the same rule bites harder here, because a credit conclusion presented as a field is a conclusion the reader will not re-derive.

**The one permitted comparison**, and it is permitted because its product is a sentence about *this* filing rather than a figure: when `total_assets` and `total_equity_and_liabilities` are both present and differ, the block appends a `notes` sentence naming both values and the gap. That is a measurement of the register's own internal consistency — the honest version of a warning that would otherwise be generic prose on every block. It never edits, reconciles or drops either number.

**(f) An absent line is not a zero, and the register is inconsistent about which one it publishes.** This is the finding that most changes what a caller may do with the block, and it was invisible until the wire was read at scale.
