The nested numeric objects are **frequently empty objects** rather than absent numbers: `langsiktigGjeld: {}` on 164 of 358, `finanskostnad: {}` on 120, `finansinntekt: {}` on 98, `innskuttEgenkapital: {}` on 101, `anleggsmidler: {}` and `opptjentEgenkapital: {}` on 64 each, and — the one that matters most — **`driftsinntekter: {}` on 51 of 358, so 14% of Norwegian filings publish no turnover at all.** Only three numbers were present on every single response: `sumEiendeler`, `sumEgenkapitalGjeld` and `aarsresultat`.

An empty object maps to `None`, never to `0.0`. That is D-004's "unknown is `None`, never `0`", and here it is provably not pedantry: two holding companies with no turnover, read on the same afternoon, publish it two different ways — `921378963` sends `sumDriftsinntekter: 0.0`, an explicit zero, while `936134610` sends `driftsinntekter: {}` beside `driftsresultat: −3,999` and `sumDriftskostnad: 3,999`, arithmetic that implies a revenue of zero the register declined to state. So `None` in this block does not even cleanly mean *unknown*: it means **the register did not publish this line for this filing, and the register is inconsistent about when it states a zero.** Every numeric field description must say exactly that, in those terms. Nothing anywhere may infer a zero from an absence, and nothing may infer an absence from a zero.

**(g) The four flags, ruled by what the wire actually shows — and one of them is not what its name promises.**

* **`small_entity`** ← `regnkapsprinsipper.smaaForetak`. True on **315 of 358** filings. This is the *majority* case, not an exception: a *lite foretak* under regnskapsloven § 1-6 files under the reduced regime, with fewer notes, no cash-flow statement and simplified valuation options permitted. It is **not** a distress signal and its description must not read as one; it is a **disclosure** caveat — fewer figures exist, and those that do were prepared under rules that permit simplification. A `notes` sentence when True.
* **`audit_exempt`** ← `revisjon.fravalgRevisjon`. True on **65 of 358** (18%). The company has resolved to opt out of audit — lawful below the aksjeloven § 7-6 thresholds. The consequence a credit decision must weigh is plain and belongs in a note when True: **no independent auditor checked these figures.** That is a real limit on the weight a caller may put on them, and it is the answer to the question this entry was asked.
* **`unaudited`** ← `revisjon.ikkeRevidertAarsregnskap`, **relayed uninverted and under a name that claims no more than the register does.** It was `True` on **0 of 573** responses — including all 65 filings by companies that had opted out of audit, which reported `ikkeRevidertAarsregnskap: false`. So the flag does **not** mean "these accounts were not audited" in the way its name suggests, or it is simply unpopulated; either way **its semantics are unverified**, and the field description must say so in one sentence: never observed `True`, meaning not vouched for, do not read a `False` here as an assertion that the accounts were audited. *Declined: a derived `audited: bool`.* It would have to resolve `fravalgRevisjon: true` against `ikkeRevidertAarsregnskap: false` — a combination observed 65 times and interpretable in two opposite ways — by picking one. D-011 and D-025(d) both forbid it, and D-009 forbids guessing which.
* **`liquidation_basis`** ← `avviklingsregnskap`. True on **3 of 215** entities the register marks `underAvvikling`, and 0 of the general 358 — rare, real, and the one flag here with a hard consequence. These are *avviklingsregnskap* under aksjeloven § 16-10: accounts prepared for the winding-up, on a realisation rather than a going-concern basis, over a final stub period — `998575133`'s runs 2026-01-01 to 2026-04-30. A `notes` sentence when True, saying the figures are not going-concern figures and the period is a final one; it must **not** restate the winding-up itself, which `CompanyStatus` already carries on the company record from the first round trip.

**(h) One fetch, two blocks — this sharpens D-041(c) and corrects D-042(j).** `filings` and `financials` for Norway are **the same URL and the same JSON body**. D-041(c)'s discipline is *one **fetch**, one `SourceRef` — not one organisation*; read literally, which is how it was written, one fetch that produces two blocks yields **one** `SourceRef`, and four rules follow.

1. **There is one cache entry, keyed on the call and not on the block** — `NO:brreg:accounts:{orgnr}`, the key `registries/no/client.py` already builds. **D-042(j)'s "one key per attachment" is corrected to one key per upstream call.** Two keys over one URL would double-store the same body and let the two blocks age apart.
2. **A lookup requesting both makes exactly one upstream request**, and the two blocks carry `SourceRef`s **equal in all five fields** — same `source`, `source_url`, `license`, `fetched_at`, `cached`. Two `fetched_at` values from one round trip would be the provenance lie D-041(c) exists to prevent. *Declined: giving each block its own `SourceRef` for symmetry with GB.* Symmetry with a different register is not a reason to describe one request as two.
3. **This is an implementation hazard, not only a rule.** `lookup_with` runs attachment methods with bounded concurrency (D-042(b)), so `BrregRegistry.filings()` and `BrregRegistry.financials()` each calling `fetch_accounts()` will **race on a cold cache and make two requests**. The two must share one in-flight result. T38 asserts the single request rather than assuming it, the way T37 asserts the default lookup makes exactly one.
4. **The two blocks may never disagree about the period.** `FilingHistory.financial_year_end`, `FiledDocument.period_end` and `FinancialPeriod.period_end` are all `regnskapsperiode.tilDato` from one body; `FinancialPeriod.document_id` and `FiledDocument.document_id` are both `journalnr`. A test asserts the equality, and it is what lets a caller join the blocks without inferring anything.

Each block still carries its own caveats **in full**, including the preview disclaimer and the only-the-latest-period sentence that `filings` already carries. Duplication across two blocks of one payload is accepted and intended: a block must be readable alone, because a caller may have asked for only one.

**(i) Total asymmetry — what a caller sees, and one rule this entry adds to D-042(g).** Norway fills this block; **Sweden and Britain will not, and the reason is ours, not theirs.** Bolagsverket's figures exist only inside the `/dokument/{id}` zip that D-041(g) ruled out; Companies House's exist only inside filed iXBRL that D-042 and `tasks/T37.md` both put out of scope on purpose. So this is not D-042(g)'s comfortable case of *one filler now, three later* — it is one filler, with the other two blocked by a scope decision we made.

What a caller sees is settled and does not change: **`include=["financials"]` on GB or SE is `bad_request` whose `hint` names that country's allowed set** (D-042(d)(3)), never a silently ignored value and never an empty block. *Declined: an empty `financials` block for a country that does not declare it.* It would mean *we cannot see figures here* and *this company reported none* at once — the collapse (b) already refused. Discoverability is paid where D-042(d) pays it: `CountryInfo.supported_includes` via `list_countries`, one round trip, no upstream call.

The claim in D-042(g) that a one-country attachment is *"expected and fine"* survives this harder case, and the reason is that `charges` already ships exactly this way — GB-only — and `search_company` has been `not_implemented` for Sweden since launch. A country-shaped capability boundary is already this product's shape. **But D-042(g) is amended by one rule it lacks, because the asymmetry here is caused by us:**

> **An attachment declared by exactly one country ships only if the reason each other live country does not declare it is recorded in this file; and where that reason is our own scope decision rather than the register's silence, the prose an agent reads must say so.**

That is D-023(c) — *a gap we can close must not be described as a boundary we cannot* — applied to a capability instead of a field. It is the mistake D-041(a) found already shipped in Sweden's prose, in seven strings, and this is the rule that stops it recurring. Concretely: no string anywhere may say "Companies House does not publish financial figures". The true sentence is that this API does not parse filed accounts documents, which is where Britain's and Sweden's figures live.

**(j) The deadline ladder does not move, and nothing here reopens it.** Ruled explicitly so it is not re-litigated. Norway's move from D-041(e) rung 3 to rung 2 is driven by **`filings`** — `FilingHistory.financial_year_end`, from `regnskapsperiode.tilDato` — and by nothing in this block. **`financials` is never an input to `Registry.deadlines`**, which stays the pure function of `(report, today)` D-018 requires; `DeadlineReport` does not gain the block (D-041(b)); and a caller who asks for `financials` without `filings` gets no deadline improvement, which is correct rather than a defect. `FinancialPeriod` carries `period_start` and `period_end` because a figure without its period is the same trap as a figure without its currency, not because any date is computed from them. **`CompanyReport.last_annual_accounts_year` continues to be filled for Norway from `/enheter`'s `sisteInnsendteAarsregnskap` — one round trip — and is never filled from either attachment** (D-041(c)).

One forward constraint, for whoever lands D-041(b)'s `include` on `company_deadlines`: **that operation accepts only include values that can change a date — `{"filings"}` today.** `financials` there is `bad_request` naming the operation's allowed set. `CountryInfo.supported_includes` keeps listing the country's full set, because it describes the country and not the operation.

**(k) Ship it, and it is `R-5f` / `tasks/T38.md`.** The case against was taken seriously and loses: a block one country fills, that a caller may not compare across borders, could easily be worth less than it costs. It is worth more, for four reasons that are all cost or evidence rather than enthusiasm. It costs **zero** additional upstream requests when co-requested with `filings` and one — the same one — when asked alone, so the marginal cost of the capability is a model and a mapper. It is free, keyless and person-clean, needing no credential and no D-028 precondition. It answers a question the research library ranked independently and in the caller's own words (`research/07-product-improvements.md` item 14, *"is this supplier solvent?"*), and `research/05-competitors.md` records that the servers with real usage return a judgement rather than a record. And it is Norway, which is where this product's users are.

**Sequencing.** `R-5f`, after `R-5d` (Norway's `filings`), taking a letter under R-5 for D-041(i)'s reason: renumbering falsifies every "Applies to tasks" line in this file. It does **not** go behind R-5e (GB insolvency), which is independent. **Because of (h), R-5d and R-5f wire as one commit if R-5d has not already been wired when T38 starts**: the one-fetch-two-blocks invariant is not assertable with one block present, and wiring them apart would make the second wiring reopen the first's cache key. That is smaller total work, not larger. R-6's 1 January 2027 clock is untouched.
Reason: the question looked like a scope call and turned out to be the sixth appearance of one invariant. D-011 — *a field whose job is to distinguish two states must not have a third that silently means both* — is what makes this a separate block rather than nineteen new fields, because on a shared `FiledDocument` a `None` turnover would mean "Britain publishes no figures" and "this Norwegian company reported no turnover line" in the same slot; and it is what makes the figures themselves survivable, because the wire proves the second state is 14% of Norwegian filings and that the register cannot even keep an explicit zero and an absent line straight. The other half of the entry is the thing this project has always claimed and had not yet been forced to prove: a value carries its provenance. A number carries more than a source — it carries a currency, a framework, an audit status and a size regime, and the honest way to say so is to make a currency-less figure impossible to construct rather than merely inadvisable. What the sweep added that no amount of reasoning would have is that the register relays filings that do not balance, some by a factor of twenty; that is the fact which turns "we should not compute ratios" from a preference into a rule.
Applies to tasks: new **R-5f / `tasks/T38.md`** (this entry), R-5d (Norway's `filings` — wires with R-5f per (k); `registries/no/accounts.py` keeps its `FilingHistory` stand-in unchanged), R-5b / T31b and R-5c / T37 (unaffected: `FiledDocument` is **not** widened by this entry), R-5e (unaffected); `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §1 (order); **amends D-042(g)** (a one-country attachment must record why the others do not declare it, and must not describe our own scope decision as the register's silence) and **D-042(j)** (cache key is per upstream call, not per attachment); **sharpens D-041(c)** (one fetch may serve two blocks, which then share one `SourceRef`); D-041(e)'s ladder, D-024, D-028 and D-031 are unamended and the published tool count does not move

---

### D-044 — The three attachments cross the seam: one canonical `FilingHistory` for all three countries, `filings` as one include name with per-country scope in `notes`, and the pins that held the seam shut become pins that hold it open

**Context.** Four attachments had been built behind the seam and none was reachable. `CountryInfo.supported_includes` read `['charges']` for Britain and `[]` for Norway and Sweden on the live service, while `registries/gb/filing_history.py`, `registries/gb/insolvency.py`, `registries/se/filings.py` and `registries/no/accounts.py` all existed, all had a `fetch_*` in their country's `client.py`, and all had test coverage. The gap between built and callable was the whole of the work; no upstream behaviour changes in this entry.

**(a) One canonical `FiledDocument`, and it was not a design decision — it was a measurement.** D-041(d) reserved a future `core.models.FiledDocument` and each country module wrote its own stand-in, blind to `core/models.py` on purpose. Comparing the three field-by-field before writing anything: `FiledDocument` is **identical across all three** — same ten field names, same order — and each module's `FilingProvenance` is field-for-field `SourceRef`. The only divergence in `FilingHistory` is Britain's `total_count`. So the canonical model is the British one, and `total_count` is `None` for the other two because neither register publishes a count of its own, which is D-011's ordinary meaning and not a gap.

That three independent implementations converged is evidence about the domain rather than about the implementers: a filing is a document, a period, a date and the register's own codes, in every register looked at so far. The docstrings were rewritten country-neutral when they moved — a canonical model may not say "always `None` for Britain" — but every fact in them was kept, including the count of live observations behind each claim.

**(b) One include name, `filings`, for three differently-scoped answers.** Companies House returns the whole filing history; Bolagsverket returns filed annual reports; Regnskapsregisteret returns filed annual accounts. *Declined: `filings` for Britain and `accounts` for Norway and Sweden.* National scope in a field name is exactly what D-042(g) forbids — national vocabulary lives in values, never in field names — and a caller writing one integration against three countries should not have to branch on the include name to ask the same question. **The scope difference is disclosed in the block's own `notes`, on every call, and in the agent-facing prose on `lookup_company`.** This is D-023(c) applied to a capability: the boundary is the register's, we say whose it is, and we say it where the caller is already reading.

**(c) `insolvency` ships Britain-only, and D-042(g) as amended by D-043(i) obliges this sentence.** Neither Bolagsverket nor Brønnøysundregistrene publishes an insolvency-case endpoint we have access to; that is the register's silence, not our scope decision, and so the amended rule is satisfied by recording it here. No string anywhere says those registers have nothing to say about insolvency — Norway's bankruptcy state already reaches `CompanyReport` on the first round trip.

**(d) The four tests that pinned the seam shut are replaced, not deleted.** `test_gb_supported_includes_is_charges_only`, `test_se_and_no_still_declare_no_includes` and `test_insolvency_is_not_wired_to_include_yet` existed to make the follow-up obvious — the last says so in its own docstring. Deleting them would remove the invariant along with the pin. Each was rewritten to assert the new truth, and the last became a stronger, cross-country test: **for every registered country, every declared include has a method and a matching `CompanyReport` field, and `country_info().supported_includes` equals the declared set.** `lookup_with` already raises `RuntimeError` on that misconfiguration; this asserts it statically, so adding a country or an attachment cannot quietly break the seam.

**(e) Conversion is tested against real payloads, not trusted.** `model_validate(model_dump())` is a straight copy only while the two shapes stay identical, and pydantic's `extra="forbid"` makes a *removed* field loud but an *added* one on the canonical side silent — it defaults. So the wiring tests map a real recorded fixture through each country's own mapper and compare the canonical block field-by-field against it. A second test covers the risk no country-module test can: **a method named `filings` that filled `charges` would pass every unit test in its own module**, so `lookup_with`'s routing is asserted per country per attachment.

**(f) The practitioner bar is asserted on both sides of the seam.** D-042(e)(2) bars relaying insolvency practitioners' names and addresses, and `insolvency.strip_practitioners` enforces it in the mapper. The canonical `InsolvencyCase` and `InsolvencyEvent` are now *also* the enforcement: neither has a field a practitioner particular could land in, so a future mapper that stopped stripping would raise `extra="forbid"` rather than relay. That is asserted, not assumed.

**(g) Two disclosures a caller can act wrongly on, and both are in the models rather than only in prose.** Companies House's insolvency 404 is the normal answer for a solvent company and is byte-identical to its answer for a number that was never issued, so a present block with `cases: []` is never evidence the entity exists. And `is_liquidation: true` is not distress: a members' voluntary liquidation is a *solvent* wind-up, 56 of the 1,485 live cases behind the lookup table were exactly that. Both are in the field descriptions the JSON schema publishes, in the block's `notes`, and in the `lookup_company` docstring an agent reads before choosing.

Reason: the work was mechanical and the risk was not. Every defect this could have shipped is silent — a block routed to the wrong field, a field dropped by a copy that stopped being a copy, a practitioner name reaching a caller because the only thing stopping it was a mapper nobody re-read. Each is now a test rather than a claim. The one genuine design question, whether three registers publishing three different subsets should share one include name, is settled the way D-042(g) already settles national vocabulary, and the honesty it costs is paid in `notes` on every single call rather than in a docstring the caller never sees.
Applies to tasks: R-5b / T31b (Sweden's `filings`, wired), R-5c / T37 (GB `filings`, wired; `charges` unchanged), R-5d (Norway's `filings`, wired), R-5e (GB `insolvency`, wired); **satisfies D-041(d)**'s reserved `core.models.FiledDocument`/`FilingHistory`; **satisfies D-043(i)**'s recording obligation for the Britain-only `insolvency`; D-011, D-023(c), D-028, D-042(b),(d),(e),(g) unamended and applied; **D-043 / T38 (`financials`) is unaffected and still open** — D-043(h)'s one-fetch-two-blocks invariant now has Norway's `filings` present to assert against, which is the condition (k) named

---

### D-045 — The charge shape is ruled rather than inherited; the LEI is the first attachment every country declares, so it lives on the base and not in three modules; and two sources that looked free are refused on their own published terms
Date: 2026-09-08
Decision: closes S-series findings 4 and 7 and the D-044-wiring review's item "decide S4 with the architect"; rules the next tranche of `~/mcp-growth/DEPTH.md`'s ranked candidates in or out; and specifies R-5's LEI, which is the one roadmap item the whole depth track was gated on and which still has no code. Eight parts. It amends **D-042(j)** and **D-026(c)** by name, sharpens **D-041(g)** and **D-042(e)**, and corrects one sentence of `legal/terms.md`. D-024, D-028 and D-031 are unamended and the published tool count does not move.

**Verified live and keyless on 2026-09-08**, and every number below that is not from a committed fixture comes from one of these. **GLEIF** (`api.gleif.org`): the complete registration-authority list, 1,071 rows over five pages, plus the three RA records by id; and about twenty `lei-records` queries. **VIES** (`ec.europa.eu/taxation_customs/vies`): the REST `check-vat` endpoint for `SE`, `GB`, `NO` and `XI`, the `check-status` endpoint, and the site's own English string bundle, which is where its disclaimer actually lives. **The Gazette** (`thegazette.co.uk`): `/fair-use-policy`, `/data` and `/robots.txt`. **Not called, and nothing below rests on an unverified claim about them:** `api.foretak.dev`, Companies House, Bolagsverket, the FCA register, and any Peppol SMP or the Peppol Directory. Charge figures are counted from the four committed fixtures (`ch_00000006`, `ch_00445790`, `ch_OC303675`, `ch_SC090312`), **110 items**, not from a live call.

**(a) The charge shape: thirteen field names were an implementer's proposal under an architect's citation, and the ruling is not a rubber stamp — one field is struck, one is added, three are added back, and both free-text fields get the treatment `persons_entitled` got.** S-series finding 4 is right on the facts: `DECISIONS.md` contains none of `charge_id`, `charge_number`, `created_on`, `delivered_on`, `satisfied_on`, `classification`, `assets_charged`, `obligations_secured`, `contains_floating_charge`, `satisfied_count` or `outstanding_count`, and D-042(h) rules `FiledDocument`, not a charge. Only `parties_entitled` (D-042(e)(3)), `is_outstanding` (D-042(j)) and `total_count` (D-042(j)) were ever ruled. The list is now ruled, name by name, against D-042(g)'s anti-bend rule and D-011.

**The window, stated first because it decides how much this costs, and stated only as far as the evidence goes.** `git show v0.3.0:src/registry_mcp/core/models.py | grep -c "class Charge"` is **0**; `class ChargeBlock` first appears in `3ecc369`, after the tag; `charges` is absent from `CHANGELOG.md`'s `[0.3.0]` and `[Unreleased]` is empty; `pyproject.toml` still reads `0.3.0` and no tag exists past `v0.3.0`. **So no published artefact has ever carried a `Charge` — not PyPI, not npm, not the MCP registry** (the D-044-wiring review's finding 7 records that PyPI's 0.3.0 lacks even what the hosted service has). **What is *not* established, and must not be assumed:** whether `api.foretak.dev` has served `include=["charges"]` since `3ecc369`. The S-series review verified production at `48cb6ab` returning `supported_includes: []`; the D-044-wiring review verified only that production had not reached `e03a518`; the deploy in between is unrecorded, and this entry was written without calling the service. **The orchestrator settles it with one request** — `GET /v1/countries`, or `GET /v1/GB/company/00445790?include=charges` — before the task lands. If the block has never been served, this is a change to an unreleased shape and costs nothing; if it has, it is a same-day correction to an unversioned deployment, which is the cheapest kind of breaking change there is and is still worth taking. Either way the price rises at the next deploy, which is the argument for doing it in the next round rather than after.

*Accepted unchanged:* `status` (the register's own word, verbatim, D-042(g)), `is_outstanding`, `parties_entitled`, `total_count`, `classification` (English, generic, and it is the register's own description of the instrument kind — its description must say it is prose, not a code, and must not be confused with `FiledDocument.type_code`), `charge_id` (the `document_id` precedent from D-041(d) and D-043(c): an opaque upstream handle carried but not fetchable; `charge_code` is absent on **19 of 110** items, all pre-2013, so `None` is honest and common), `charge_number` (the register's per-company sequence; its description must say it is not unique across companies and is not a lookup key, because a field named `..._number` beside one named `..._id` is otherwise a coin flip for an agent).

*Accepted, with the description corrected:* `created_on`, `delivered_on`, `satisfied_on`. The names stand — they are the register's own English, they are generic, and they match `InsolvencyEvent.occurred_on`, which shipped in the same commit; renaming three fields to the older `..._at` convention would leave `occurred_on` inconsistent and buy nothing. But **`created_on` is currently described as "When the charge was created", which an agent will read as a record timestamp.** It is the date the *charge instrument* was created; `delivered_on` is the register's receipt date; and the interval between them is the Companies Act 2006 s.859A window in which a charge must be delivered to be registered at all. That is a credit fact, and the descriptions must carry it. *Declined: a derived `days_to_delivery` or a `registered_late` flag.* D-041(d)'s refusal of `filed_late: bool` applies verbatim — the 21-day period admits a court extension under s.859F, so a boolean is a legal conclusion, and unlike `days_from_fee_point` there is no reason to precompute a subtraction of two fields the caller already has.

*Struck: `outstanding_count`.* It is not a register figure. It is `total_count - satisfied_count` — our arithmetic — sitting between two fields that *are* the register's own, under a name that does not say so, and its own description already concedes the ambiguity: *"a register that also tracks a distinct partially-satisfied state folds it in here as not-fully-satisfied."* That sentence is the defect. On a **shared, country-neutral** model whose stated purpose is that Norway's Løsøreregisteret and Sweden's företagsinteckningar fill it later, one field would mean "outstanding" for a register with no part-satisfied concept and "not fully satisfied" for one that has it — **two meanings decided by which country filled it**, which is the collapse this file has now refused seven times (D-011, D-026(b), D-028(3), D-041(d), D-042(d)(3), D-043(b), and here). Second, independent reason: `total_count` is a whole-company figure and `charges` is one page — NatWest Markets (`SC090312`) reports `total_count` 137 with 100 items returned — so `outstanding_count` would be 110 beside a `charges` list containing 77 outstanding rows, two numbers that legitimately disagree with nothing in the payload saying why. Striking it costs the caller one subtraction and buys them knowing which subtraction they did.

*Added: `part_satisfied_count: int | None`.* Companies House publishes it, in every one of the four fixtures, as a whole-company figure identical in kind to `total_count` and `satisfied_count`. D-041(g)'s reasoning applies without modification — *"the list is already on the wire, so discarding it would be a second decision to reopen later"* — and its absence is precisely what forced `outstanding_count` to be ambiguous. It was **0 in all four payloads**, so its description must say that this project has never observed a non-zero value and that the count is the register's own, not derived.

*Added: `contains_fixed_charge: bool | None` and `contains_negative_pledge: bool | None`, beside the existing `contains_floating_charge`.* The module declined them on the correct ground — D-042(g) reserves a widening to this file — and this file rules them in. Counted over the 110 committed items: `contains_fixed_charge` **86**, `contains_negative_pledge` **59**, `contains_floating_charge` **18**. So the block today carries the *rarest* of the three, and DEPTH.md §6 names the one it omits as the fact a lender acts on: *"`contains_negative_pledge` alone is a fact a lender changes behaviour on."* D-042(g)'s anti-bend rule is satisfied by disclosure rather than by omission: all three are common-law security concepts, Companies House is the only register we have seen publish them, and each description must say so and must name Løsøreregisteret's and företagsinteckningar's different taxonomies as the reason another filler may leave them `None` forever. That is the `days_from_fee_point` precedent.

**And the finding that makes all three flags dangerous if copied without reading: the register emits each key only when it is `True`.** Across 110 items, `contains_fixed_charge`, `contains_negative_pledge` and `contains_floating_charge` were **`True` on every occurrence and `False` on none** — the key is simply absent otherwise. So the mapper's `particulars.get(...)` yields `None` for 92 of 110 floating-charge readings, and `None` there does **not** mean "we do not know": it means *the register did not mark this instrument*, which is not the same as *this instrument does not contain one*, because `particulars` is a summary and only 47 of 110 items carry any `description` at all. Each description must say that in those words. **Nothing may convert the absence into `False`** — D-011, and D-043(f)'s "an absent line is not a zero" in a different register.

*Not added, named so they are not re-derived:* `floating_charge_covers_all` and `chargor_acting_as_bare_trustee` (CH publishes both; **0 of 110** observed, so each would be a field that is permanently `None` and asserts nothing — D-043(c)'s reason for declining `oppstillingsplan`, and D-009); `assets_ceased_released` (**5 of 110**, all `whole-property-released`; a real state, but it duplicates what `satisfied_on` and `status` already tell a caller, and one value is not a vocabulary); `scottish_alterations` (**2 of 110**, `has_restricting_provisions`; sub-national vocabulary on a shared model, and D-027 puts sub-national facts in a *field on the record*, which this is not).

**S-series finding 7 — the free text — is ruled the way D-042(e)(3) ruled `persons_entitled`: relayed, typed, bound, and disclosed. Not dropped, and not left as it is.** The reviewer scanned all 110 items for personal titles and found none, and that is worth having; it is also not the finding. Reading the values themselves: 47 items carry `particulars.description` (longest **260** characters, mean 90) and 19 carry `secured_details.description` (longest **243**, mean 93), and one of them relays, verbatim, *"the euro designated account in the name of the company with sort code 40-05-15 account number 67851117"*. That is a live bank account identifier in a field whose schema constrains nothing, and it is exactly the class of surprise D-042(e)(1) recorded for `description_values` — arriving in the tranche D-042(e) had already pronounced clean. Four rulings:

1. **Both texts are relayed, verbatim, uncapped.** *Declined: dropping them.* The description of what is charged is the substance of the block (DEPTH.md §6), and the sort code is the *company's* account on the public register — redacting it is over-compliance of exactly the kind D-026(b) refused, and it would break the "we relay what the register publishes" contract to hide a fact the register publishes. *Declined: a length cap.* The observed maximum is 260 characters against a page size of 100, so a cap invents a number to solve a problem the data does not have, and truncating a security description mid-clause is worse than its length.
2. **Both texts gain a sibling carrying the register's own type token, verbatim: `assets_charged_type` and `obligations_secured_type`.** This is not tidiness. `particulars.type` is `short-particulars` (19) or `brief-description` (28) — two different kinds of text under one field name — and `secured_details.type` was **`amount-secured` on 19 of 19**, while Companies House's own enumeration distinguishes `amount-secured` from `obligations-secured`. **So the field named `obligations_secured` has never once carried an obligations-secured value.** That is a 100% mis-assertion rate on observed data, and it is precisely the argument D-042(e)(3) used to rename `persons_entitled`: the name asserts something the register does not. The cheaper fix than a rename is the one this file already prefers — national vocabulary in the *value*, in a sibling field, exactly as `status` sits beside `is_outstanding` and `accounting_framework` beside `scope`.
3. **D-028(1)'s binding extends from `parties_entitled` to both texts**, in the same words: never a lookup key, never indexed, never searchable, never in a log line (D-040). And **`legal/terms.md` is factually wrong as it stands** — D-042(e)(3) had it say `parties_entitled` is "the one place a natural person's name can reach a response", and free prose describing charged property can name a guarantor or a charged dwelling. The sentence must name all three fields. This is a correction to a shipped legal document, not an addition.
4. **One unconditional `notes` sentence on every block that carries either text**, saying it is the register's own free prose, relayed verbatim and not parsed, and that it may contain particulars of property, account details or a natural person's name. D-044(b)'s disclosure pattern, for the same reason: the caller is told where they are reading, not in a docstring they never see.

**Footprint and sequencing.** The edit is `core/models.py` (`Charge` at ~:600, `ChargeBlock` at ~:685 — one struck field, four added, six descriptions rewritten), `registries/gb/charges.py` (the mapper, and the eight false "D-042(h)" citations), `legal/terms.md` (one sentence), and the GB charge tests. **It runs after `tasks/T40.md` merges, and T40 shrinks it:** T40 folds `gb/charges.py`'s stand-in `Charge`/`ChargeBlock`/`ChargeProvenance` into `core.models` and deletes them, so the four new fields are added once rather than twice and the eight citations may already be gone — read the file before sizing the task rather than trusting this sentence. It is one task, it belongs to **the next round**, and it must land **before the next deploy** for the reason in the second paragraph. This entry does not assign it a number; the two `core/models.py` docstring citations that attributed the shape to D-042(g),(h) are corrected to cite D-045(a) here, so that nothing in the tree claims an authority that does not exist while the task waits.

**(b) VIES is refused, and not on the privacy ground DEPTH.md expected — on its own published terms, which forbid what this product is.** DEPTH.md §5 ranks VIES sixth, *"Effort: S. Privacy: none"*, as the call that would turn `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §2.6's honest `vat_registered: null` into an answer. Both halves of that assessment are wrong, and one live call and one string bundle settle it.

**The terms.** VIES's own disclaimer, fetched verbatim from the service's English bundle: the site exists *"to allow persons involved in the intra-Community supply of goods or of services to obtain confirmation of the validity of the VAT identification number of any specified person, in accordance to article 31 of Council Regulation (EC) No. 904/2010"*, and then — *"**Any other use and any extraction and use of the data which is not in conformity with the objective of this site is strictly forbidden. Any retransmission of the contents of this site, whether for a commercial purpose or otherwise, as well as any more general use other than as far as is necessary to support the activity of a legitimate user (for example: to draw up their own invoices) is expressly forbidden. In addition, any copying or reproduction of the contents of this site is strictly forbidden.**"* Retransmitting upstream content to third-party callers under a stated licence, and caching it, is a fair description of this entire product. There is no reading of that paragraph under which a public MCP server may proxy VIES on behalf of unknown callers. **A source whose terms forbid re-use cannot be given a `SourceRef.license`**, and a project whose differentiator is a machine-readable licence field may not be the one that shrugs at this.

**The personal data, which is worse than the sole-trader hazard the brief anticipated.** The hazard on the table was that a Swedish sole trader's VAT number is `SE` + personnummer + `01`, so VIES would return a natural person's name and address for an identifier `id_may_be_personal` flags. That is real. It is not the whole risk: `GET /rest-api/ms/SE/vat/556016068001` — a *large listed company* — returns `"address": "c/o KI/EAB/CX  ANNELIE ANDERSSON \nTORSHAMNSGATAN 23 \n164 83 STOCKHOLM"`. **The name of a natural person, in the address of a public company, on the first call made.** D-042(e)'s field-level test — *a payload is person-bearing if any field in its schema can carry a natural person's name or any other particular of one* — is failed by `address` for every registrant, not only for flagged ones. So the mitigation the brief sketched (carry the yes/no and nothing else for flagged identifiers) is the wrong shape: the correct minimisation is to carry `name` and `address` for **nobody**, at which point the block is one boolean, and a block that is one boolean does not justify a second upstream, a cache kind, a provenance object and a licence problem.

**Two further reasons, each sufficient on its own.** `GET /ms/GB/vat/…` and `GET /ms/NO/vat/…` both return **HTTP 200** with `isValid: false` and `userError: "INVALID_INPUT"` — so an implementation that reads `isValid` would report `vat_registered: false` for every British and Norwegian company, which is a false negative dressed as an answer, and the collapse D-011 exists to prevent. VIES's own `check-status` endpoint lists **28 jurisdictions** — the 27 member states plus `XI` — and neither `GB` nor `NO` is among them; two of the 28 (`BE`, `EL`) were reporting `Unavailable` at the moment it was read, so even for Sweden a null is routinely the member state's downtime rather than a fact about the company.

**So: no `vat` include, no VIES block, no brief.** Sweden's `vat_registered` stays `None` permanently, and `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` §2.6's sentence is correct as it stands. **What changes instead is one hint, and it is the only use of VIES compatible with the paragraph above: we point, we do not proxy.** Where a caller reads a null `vat_registered` for an EU country, the `notes` sentence may name VIES as the place the *caller themselves* may confirm a VAT number for their own supply — which is precisely what Article 31 provides for, and which D-007's hint rule already asks of us. Nothing is fetched, nothing is cached, nothing is relayed. *Declined: asking the Commission for permission.* It is a reasonable thing for a person to do and it is not an architect's decision; recorded so it is a choice Kim can make rather than a door this entry closed. *Declined: Skatteverket instead.* DEPTH.md §4.2: contract-gated, key assigned after a contract, "organisational legitimation" for some APIs — an organisational L, not a task.

**(c) The FCA Financial Services Register is out for the next round, and the mechanism it would need is ruled now so that ruling it in later is a task rather than an entry.** DEPTH.md §5 ranks it fourth and the strongest thing in its favour is not ours: Companies House's own `partial_data_available` enum carries `'full-data-available-from-financial-conduct-authority'`, i.e. the register naming its own successor. It is out for three reasons that are about sequencing rather than merit. It needs a **new credential** on a country that already needs one, and every credential is an operator's problem before it is ours (D-017, D-037). Its value is concentrated in a sub-population — authorised financial firms — which is a small share of the counterparties this product is asked about, while the next round's three tasks all serve every lookup. And DEPTH.md marks its endpoint, its headers and its 50-requests-per-10-seconds limit `[search]`, so ruling it in today would mean writing an unverified endpoint into a brief, which is the one thing D-043's live sweep was worth doing to avoid.

**Ruled now, because it is the general question and it will recur:** a country module may need a **second** credential for an attachment, and D-017's contract has one slot. D-037 already met the two-secret problem for Sweden and solved it with *one pointer plus a complete hint*, explicitly declining to widen `api_key_env` to a tuple and naming the additive fix for when a third case arrives. This is that case, and the additive fix is the right one, with one change: the natural shape is **per-attachment**, not per-registry. `Registry` gains `include_api_key_env: ClassVar[Mapping[str, str]] = {}` — `include` value → environment variable name — and `CountryInfo` publishes it, so `list_countries` answers "the base lookup needs `COMPANIES_HOUSE_API_KEY`; the `fca` attachment needs `FCA_API_KEY` as well" in one round trip, which is D-017's whole purpose and D-042(d)(1)'s discoverability rule. `requires_api_key` and `api_key_env` keep meaning **the base lookup**, unchanged, because that is what every existing consumer reads them as. An attachment whose credential is unset raises `upstream_error` with a hint naming its own variable, exactly as D-017 requires of the base — the block goes absent and the lookup still succeeds (D-042(b)). **Firm endpoints only, individuals barred by D-028 regardless of what the licence permits**, and the licence is the fourth unread fact.

**(d) The Gazette is out, and the reason is not the rate limit and not the 9pm–7am clause — it is that the Gazette's own `robots.txt` disallows every machine-readable representation of a notice.** DEPTH.md §7(4) left the crawling-window question open and correctly said it is a question for the Gazette rather than for inference. It does not have to be asked, because the fair-use policy makes a second document binding and that document answers first.

The fair-use policy, verbatim: *"If you create a bot, crawler, scraper or **other automated tool that accesses our website or APIs**, you must: Carry out crawling in non-business hours between 9pm and 7am UK. Limit requests to 5 request every 10 seconds. **Comply with our `robots.txt` file and any crawl delay instructions.** …"* An MCP server calling the Gazette on demand is unambiguously an "other automated tool that accesses our APIs", so every obligation in that list applies to it, `robots.txt` included. And `https://www.thegazette.co.uk/robots.txt` — 6,179 lines, `Crawl-delay: 10`, `User-agent: *` — carries these five rules among its wildcards:

```
Disallow: /notice/*/data.xml
Disallow: /notice/*/data.pdf
Disallow: /notice/*/data.rdf
Disallow: /notice/*/data.ttl
Disallow: /notice/*/data.jsonld
```

**Every machine-readable form of an individual notice is disallowed to automated tools** — including the JSON-LD record that is the only thing a notice block could carry, and that `bouch`'s `gazette_notice` tool returns. The search feed is not disallowed and stays reachable, but an index entry without the notice behind it is a headline and a date, which is strictly less than the case types and dated events `include=["insolvency"]` already returns for GB since `e03a518`. So the question "is an on-demand lookup crawling?" never arises: the representation we would consume is disallowed at every hour, and `Crawl-delay: 10` is in any case stricter than the policy's own five-per-ten-seconds for anything robots.txt covers.

The licence adds a second reason that would bite even with permission. The Gazette's own re-use page: the data *"is Crown Copyright and is therefore free for you to use under the Open Government Licence. **However, please note that this licence does not cover the re-use of personal data.**"* The Gazette publishes personal insolvency beside corporate insolvency, so the block would need a personal/corporate split enforced at the field level before a `SourceRef.license` could honestly say "OGL" — and D-042(e)(1) has already shown, twice, that a per-field split in someone else's free text is where this project's surprises live. **Not now**, and the one thing that would change it is a written answer from the Gazette naming on-demand single lookups, which is an email and not an inference.

**(e) The LEI: D-026(c)'s shape survives unamended, GLEIF's own query is not what a reader would guess, and the mechanism is a base-class attachment — because an attachment every country declares does not belong in every country's module.**

**Nothing in D-042, D-043 or D-044 changed R-5's LEI.** `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §7's `LeiRecord`, its 7-day TTL and its done-check are confirmed as written. `SourceRef` shipped unamended (`core/models.py:561`), which was D-041(c)'s stated test of whether D-026(c) got the shape right, and it passed. The two-level nullability is D-026(c)'s and is unchanged: **absent block** = not asked for, or the fetch failed and a `notes` sentence says so; **present block with `lei=None`** = GLEIF holds no LEI for this entity, which is a real answer about a counterparty.

**GLEIF's registration-authority codes, verified against GLEIF's own list — and the brief's three codes are two-thirds right, in a way that matters.** Fetching all 1,071 rows: `RA000472` is **Foretaksregisteret** and `RA000473` is **Enhetsregisteret**, both Brønnøysundregistrene, and our lookup reads Enhetsregisteret while every observed Norwegian LEI record cites **RA000472**; `RA000544` is Bolagsverket, correct and unique for Sweden; and `RA000585` is **Companies House, England and Wales only** — Northern Ireland is `RA000586` and Scotland is `RA000587`, confirmed live on NatWest Markets (`SC090312` → `RA000587`). A brief that pinned `RA000585` for "the UK" would have produced a test that fails on every Scottish company.

**So the RA code is a verification, not a selector, and there is no RA table in `core/`.** The query that works, verified on Equinor, DNB, Telenor, Ericsson, Tesco, NatWest Markets, BP and Carillion, is `filter[entity.jurisdiction]` — our own ISO country code, free, already on the contract — **and** `filter[entity.registeredAs]`. The RA code is then *carried* from the record GLEIF returns (`entity.registeredAt.id`, beside `entity.registeredAs`), so a caller can see which register GLEIF says the entity is registered in, and a test asserts the observed values rather than the query depending on them. That is D-018's "say which source said what" applied to a third party's opinion about a second party's register.

**The trap, and it is the single thing an implementer will get wrong: `entity.registeredAs` is an exact-match filter on the string the register writes, not on the identifier we normalise.** Measured:

| | our normalised `id` | GLEIF `registeredAs` | bare-digit query | formatted query |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NO Equinor | `923609016` | `"923 609 016"` | **0 hits** | 1 hit |
| NO DNB | `984851006` | `"984 851 006"` | **0 hits** | 1 hit |
| SE Ericsson | `5560160680` | `"556016-0680"` | **0 hits** | 1 hit |
| GB Tesco | `00445790` | `"00445790"` | 1 hit | — |
| GB NatWest Markets | `SC090312` | `"SC090312"` | 1 hit | — |

The rule that satisfies all five without a country table, and without editing a single country module: **the query key is `format_id(id)` when the registry defines one, and the normalised `id` otherwise.** `Registry.format_id` is already on the contract and already returns `None` by default; Norway's returns `"923 609 016"` and Sweden's returns `"556016-0680"` for the ten-digit form, which are exactly GLEIF's strings, and Britain does not override it, which is exactly what GLEIF wants. That this fell out rather than being arranged is worth one sentence: `format_id` was specified as *"the identifier as a local would write it"*, and GLEIF stores what the local register wrote. **Both candidates are tried — the formatted one first, the bare one only on zero hits — and the block's `notes` name the string that was sent**, so a miss is legible rather than silent. A twelve-digit Swedish identifier produces neither GLEIF string and returns `lei=None`; that is correct rather than a gap, because the twelve-digit form is the sole-trader form (D-039) and a sole trader has no LEI — and the note says which string was tried.

**`registration.status`, not `entity.status`, and it must not be read as distress.** Both exist on the record and they disagree routinely: Carillion plc (`03782379`), in liquidation since 2018, is `entity.status: ACTIVE` with `registration.status: LAPSED`; so is Lehman Brothers International (Europe). `LeiRecord.registration_status` is D-026(c)'s field and it carries **`registration.status`** — the state of the LEI's own maintenance, `ISSUED` / `LAPSED` / … — verbatim, national-vocabulary-in-values, with a description saying in terms that a lapsed LEI means the entity stopped renewing it and is **not** evidence of insolvency, and that GLEIF's `entity.status` is not carried because it is a claim about a company made by neither the company's register nor us. *Declined: a derived `is_active` or `lei_current: bool`.* D-025(d) and D-011: two status vocabularies, one of which we have just shown to be unreliable, do not fold into a boolean.

**The mechanism: `universal_includes` on the base, and it is accepted.** `Registry` gains a concrete `async def lei(self, id: str) -> LeiRecord` and `universal_includes: ClassVar[frozenset[str]] = frozenset({"lei"})`. Three call sites union it with the country's own set: `lookup_with`'s validation, `country_info()`'s `supported_includes`, and D-044(d)'s cross-country static test. *Declined: each country adding `"lei"` to its own `supported_includes` by hand.* Three reasons, and the first is only the cheapest. It would make the LEI task edit three `registries/*/__init__.py` files in a round where another task holds Norway's, which is a merge conflict bought for nothing. It would make a fourth country's LEI an edit rather than a default, when D-008's promise is that a country is one folder plus one import line. And it would be false to what the LEI is: every other attachment is *a country's own register*, declared by that country because only that country has it, whereas GLEIF publishes every jurisdiction from one endpoint under one licence with one TTL — a per-country declaration would be three modules asserting the same global fact, which is the "two shapes for one concept" D-042(b) declined by name.

**One country does not get it, and the rule is general rather than Swedish.** `Registry.lei()` sends the identifier to a **third-party host, in a URL query string**. For a registry with `id_may_be_personal = True` that string can be a personnummer — and D-039's exposure category is exactly *"URLs, access logs and `Referer` headers"*, the category Bolagsverket designed a POST-only API around and the category D-040(e) turned uvicorn's access log off for. D-041(g) declined a gate on Sweden's second call with the reasoning *"the identifier is already in the `POST /organisationer` body on the same TLS session to the same host, so a second POST body adds no exposure category"* — that sentence is a limit as much as a permission, and GLEIF is not the same host, not the same organisation, and not a POST. So:

> **The effective include set is `(universal_includes if not self.id_may_be_personal else frozenset()) | supported_includes`.** An attachment that sends the identifier to a third party is not offered by a registry whose identifiers can be a natural person's.

Sweden therefore does not declare `lei` today; `list_countries` says so; `include=["lei"]` for `SE` is `bad_request` naming SE's allowed set, never a silent empty block (D-042(d)(3)). This costs Sweden one attachment and it is recoverable on cheap evidence: `CompanyReport` for Sweden already carries the sole-trader signal from the *first* round trip (D-039 derives its note from `organisationsidentitet.typ.kod`), so a later entry could gate on the report rather than on the country — but `lookup_with` passes attachments an `id` and not a report (D-042(b)), so that is a `core/registry.py` change with its own entry, and it is named here so it is a decision and not a gap. *Declined: gating on digit count.* D-032 refuses to distinguish ten digits from twelve and D-040(c),(d) refuse to protect the question by inspecting its shape; blanket, by flag, is the same rule those two entries already settled twice.

**The per-kind TTL table, which D-026(c), D-028(2), D-041(h) and D-042(j) have all now named and none has built. This amends D-042(j).** The shape is `kind → (ok_ttl, empty_ttl)` in `core/cache.py`, **and `_ttl_seconds` derives the kind from the cache key rather than from a new argument.** That is not a shortcut: the kind is *already* the third `:`-separated segment of every key in the tree — `GB:companies-house:charges:{crn}`, `NO:brreg:filings:{orgnr}`, `SE:bolagsverket:filings:{env}:{id}` — because D-006's convention put it there. So **no existing caller migrates, in this task or any other**: the five sites that fake a per-kind TTL with `status="not_found"` (`gb/client.py` ×3, `se/client.py`, `no/client.py`) keep working unchanged, because a kind absent from the table falls through to today's behaviour byte for byte, and a kind present in it reads its pair. The only new row is `lei`. *Declined: adding a `kind=` argument to `cache.set`.* It would touch five country-module call sites in a round where two other tasks hold those files, to pass a value the key already carries.

**`lei` is `(7 days, 24 hours)`.** The 7 days is D-026(c)'s, unamended, for its own two reasons — an LEI renews annually and a longer TTL keeps a courtesy load off a free service. The empty TTL is **24 hours, not D-006's 1 hour**, and the asymmetry is preserved rather than abandoned: D-006 gives negatives an hour because *"a newly registered company must become visible fast"*, D-042(j) because a stale "no charges" harms a credit decision, and D-029(e) because from 2027 a stale Peppol negative makes a sender breach a statutory duty. **None of those reads across.** Nothing statutory, nothing time-critical and no credit conclusion turns on an entity acquiring an LEI, and the direction of harm from re-asking GLEIF hourly for an answer that is "no" for most of the world's companies points at the free service. **And the clamp D-028(2) requires arrives for free:** the table ignores `REGISTRY_MCP_CACHE_TTL_SECONDS` for kinds it declares, so when `officers` gets a row at one hour, an operator tuning cache performance cannot silently extend personal-data retention — which is the hard rule D-028(2) wrote and the reason it wanted a table. Say so in the docstring, so the next reader adds the row rather than a second mechanism.

**`tasks/T42.md` is written** for a Sonnet: core-only footprint, GLEIF's query pinned to the strings above, the RA codes asserted as observations rather than used as inputs, and the two-level nullability tested as such.

**(f) Peppol cannot be briefed from D-029 and §8 alone, and the missing facts are named rather than guessed. No `tasks/T43.md`.** D-029 is complete on shape, name, TTL, the offline `participant_id` and the refusal to emit a `Deadline`; `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §8 is complete on the done-check. What neither supplies is the thing D-029(d) makes load-bearing: *"prefer the authoritative resolution, allow the Directory as a fallback, and **name in `provenance.source` which one answered**."* A Sonnet cannot implement a rule about which of two routes answered without knowing either route. Exactly four facts are missing, all of them somebody else's to state, and `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §2 already lists three of them:

1. **The authoritative SMP resolution route for a `0192:` participant** — the concrete request an implementer makes, and whether it is an HTTP endpoint at all or a DNS-based discovery step; nothing in the repository names it, and the wrong guess is a Directory-only implementation reporting a lagging index as authoritative, which is the precise failure D-029(a) built the attachment to expose.
2. **The Peppol Directory's query endpoint and response shape**, for the declared fallback.
3. **Terms of use and any rate limit or registration expectation** for both, from Digdir or DFØ. Without this there is no `SourceRef.license` value, and (b) and (d) of this entry are two demonstrations in one day of why a source's own terms are not a formality.
4. **What `document_types` actually contains on the wire** — D-029(b) types it `list[str]`, and whether those strings are document-type identifiers, process identifiers or both decides whether the field is usable or decorative.

All four are settled by reading Digdir's and DFØ's own documentation and making **one** live resolution against a known ELMA participant — a recon of a few hours, and the right shape for it is D-043's: read the wire first, then brief. Until then R-6 stays where `CORE_ROADMAP_SPEC.md` §2 puts it. The 1 January 2027 clock is not threatened by one recon task.

**(g) The Swedish deadline rung: `tasks/T31.md` Part B §4–§6 is still right after D-044, one thing is needed in `core/registry.py`, and Part A's surviving false sentences are folded in.** D-044 landed the block and made `core.models.FilingHistory` canonical, so §4's premise — `deadlines_for(report, today)` reads `report.filings.financial_year_end` and stays pure (D-018) — is now true of a real field rather than a reserved one, and §4's `VERIFY` gate on Bolagsverket's own published table of *sista dag för årsstämma* is untouched: no non-December date is computed until that table is cited, and if it cannot be sourced the block ships without rung 2 (D-009). §5's three states of N9 and §6's surface work are unchanged.

**What §6 did not foresee, and it is a `core/registry.py` change.** Both surfaces today do `report = await registry.lookup(id)` then `registry.deadline_report(report, today)`. The obvious edit is `lookup_with(id, include)` in place of `lookup(id)`, and it is not sufficient, because D-043(j) added a forward constraint after T31 was written: **`company_deadlines` accepts only include values that can change a date — `{"filings"}` today — and `financials` there is `bad_request` naming *the operation's* allowed set**, while `CountryInfo.supported_includes` keeps listing the country's full set because it describes the country and not the operation. `lookup_with` validates against the country's set and cannot produce that hint. So `Registry` gains one concrete method beside `lookup_with`:

```python
async def deadline_report_with(self, id: str, include: Sequence[str], today: date) -> DeadlineReport
```

which validates `include` against `self.supported_includes & DEADLINE_INCLUDES` (a module constant in `core/registry.py`, `frozenset({"filings"})`), raises `bad_request` naming that intersection, calls `lookup_with`, and returns `deadline_report`. *Declined: validating in the two surfaces.* D-042(b)'s finding verbatim — *"written per surface it drifts within a release"* — and D-018's whole finding was a country-specific read inside a method whose contract forbade it. *Declined: an `allowed=` parameter on `lookup_with`.* It gives one method two contracts and puts the deadlines path's vocabulary into the lookup path's signature; a second concrete method beside it is the shape D-042(b) already chose for `lookup_with` itself, and both surfaces then keep doing what D-010 asks — call one method, dump one model.

**And Part A of T31 is still unapplied, six weeks of prose later.** Checked in the tree: the false claim that *"Bolagsverket's free dataset does not publish the financial year"* survives in `registries/se/rules.py` (`_CALENDAR_YEAR_NOTE` ~:674, both `_APPLIES_BECAUSE` strings ~:690 and ~:704, and `rules_markdown()` ~:900), in `SWEDEN_SPEC.md` (~:1140, ~:1153, N9 at ~:1776), in `mcp/server.py`'s `company_deadlines` description (~:504), in `static/well-known/mcp/server-card.json`, in `static/llms-full.txt` (~:442) and in `README.md:150`. The first five share T44's footprint exactly and are folded into it under D-041(a) and (f); the remaining three are generated or static surfaces and go to the surfaces-sync round, listed in T44's out-of-scope so they cannot be lost again. **`tasks/T44.md` is written** as a pointer brief.

**(h) One sentence each, so nobody re-derives them.** All cite `~/mcp-growth/DEPTH.md`.

* **HM Land Registry CCOD / OCOD** — the only source with a real company→property path, and it is out on process rather than merit: free but gated behind identity verification and licence terms that bind redistribution, which collides with relaying it onward under a stated licence (§3.2). Revisit only if a caller asks for property and Kim is willing to do the registration.
* **HM Land Registry Price Paid** — decoration: it answers "what sold near this postcode", not "what does this company own", and the category leader ships it under a name that promises otherwise (§2.3).
* **HMRC Check a UK VAT number** — v2 moved behind authentication and registration takes about two weeks, so the keyless story is gone and what remains duplicates a fact Companies House does not hold anyway (§3.2).
* **Charity Commission (E&W)** — a different population of a different kind of entity, and its profile endpoint returns trustees, which is D-028 (§3.2).
* **Employment Tribunal decisions** — barred outright, not deferred: decisions name claimants and routinely disclose health, disability, race and religion, which is Article 9 special-category data (§3.2). This is a do-not-build and needs no further entry.
* **OFSI / sanctions screening** — excluded by standing policy in `README.md` and `SECURITY.md`, and DEPTH.md §5 is right that the exclusion is worth more than the tool.
* **Norway — Løsøreregisteret** — the Norwegian analogue of GB charges and the single best thing we cannot have: Maskinporten-gated, documented for distributors and `kredittopplysningsforetak` only (§4.1). It is the named future filler for `ChargeBlock` in D-042(g) and stays exactly that.
* **Norway — Konkursregisteret** — restricted to actors with statutory authority to process fødselsnummer, with ID-porten on the quarantine search and licence "Not provided" on every distribution (§4.1). D-028(6)'s `not_implemented`-plus-hint case.
* **Norway — Kunngjøringer** — free, and the wrong shape: a daily/weekly XML subscription, not a query API, so consuming it means an ingest pipeline and a database rather than a proxy (§4.1). A different class of work from everything else on the list, and the bankruptcy state it would carry already reaches `CompanyReport` on the first round trip.
* **Norway — `/enheter/{orgnr}/roller`** and **reelle rettighetshavere** — barred by D-028 and restated by D-042(f); DEPTH.md §5.4(b) adds one rule the architect can write today without code, and it is written here: **if `/roller` is ever built, Norway's minimisation is stronger than D-028(1)'s default, not equal to it** — a fødselsnummer's first six digits are the date of birth, so a full `fødselsdato` beside a full name is materially closer to a national identifier than the UK's month-and-year, and the field is truncated to year-month at the boundary or dropped.
* **Sweden — `/dokument/{id}`** — the zip, ruled out by D-041(g) and unchanged: a third request, an XBRL package to parse, and a financial-statements feature wearing a filing-history costume (§4.2).
* **Sweden — Skatteverket** — no free, keyless, per-organisationsnummer F-skatt or moms check exists; the portal's APIs are contract-gated and some require organisational legitimation (§4.2). This is why (b)'s `vat_registered` stays `None` rather than moving to a different source.
* **Sweden — Kronofogden** — open-data pages describe statistics; there is no per-company API, and per-company payment defaults are a credit-bureau product rather than open data (§4.2).
* **GB `/registers`, `/exemptions`, `/uk-establishments`** — decoration, decoration-unless-PSC-ships, and narrow, in that order; D-042(e) already found all three clean of personal data, which is why they are a scope call and not a privacy one (§3.1, §5).
* **GB Document API** — not now: it needs filing history first, returns binary, and iXBRL parsing is a project, which is D-041(g)'s bar in a second country (§3.1).

Reason: two of these parts came out the opposite way to the ranking that prompted them, and both for the same reason — the source's own words were read rather than summarised. VIES was ranked *"Effort: S. Privacy: none"* and turns out to forbid retransmission in terms, and to return a natural person's name in the address of a listed company on the first call made; the Gazette's open question about a crawling window turns out not to need answering, because the site's own `robots.txt`, which its fair-use policy makes binding, disallows every machine-readable form of the record we would consume. That is the same discipline D-043 applied to a payload, applied to a licence, and it is cheap: three fetches settled two items that had a paragraph of reasoning each. The charge shape is the other kind of lesson. Nothing was wrong with the implementer's judgement — the module flagged its own two open questions honestly and declined to widen a shared model on its own authority, which is exactly right — but a citation nobody checked put thirteen unruled names into a country-neutral model reserved for two future fillers, and the field that would have hurt is the one that reads as a register figure and is our subtraction. What the wire added that no amount of reasoning would have is that Companies House emits its three security flags only when true, so the block's most decision-relevant fact is encoded as an absence; a model that treats absence as "we do not know" and a model that treats it as "no" are the same code and different products.
Applies to tasks: **`tasks/T42.md`** (R-5's LEI, this entry, (e)); **`tasks/T44.md`** (the Swedish rung + T31a's surviving strings, (g)); a task in the next round for (a)'s field list, footprint named in (a) and **due before the next deploy**; T38 and T45 unaffected; R-6 stays blocked with (f)'s four facts named; **amends D-042(j)** (the per-kind TTL table is keyed on the kind already in the cache key, so no caller migrates; `charges` gains `part_satisfied_count`, `contains_fixed_charge` and `contains_negative_pledge` and loses `outstanding_count`) and **D-026(c)** (the LEI is a base-class `universal_includes` attachment, not a per-country declaration; empty TTL 24 h); **sharpens D-041(g)** (D-042(e)'s person-bearing test extends to free-text particulars) and **D-042(e)** (`legal/terms.md`'s "one place a natural person's name can reach a response" is corrected to name three fields); **satisfies D-028(2)**'s clamp requirement by construction; supersedes S-series finding 4's open question and closes findings 4 and 7; **D-024, D-028, D-031 unamended and the published tool count does not move**

### D-046 — Peppol after the wire: `false` is earnable only on the SML/SMP route, `provenance.source` names the SMP the NAPTR named, the SML step buys `dnspython`, and the licence is a stated absence
Date: 2026-09-08
Decision: `tasks/T48-recon.md` read all three routes on 2026-09-08 — ~50 NAPTR resolutions, 4 SMP GETs against 2 different SMPs, 17 Peppol Directory queries, all keyless — and settled D-045(f)'s four facts. Three of them confirm D-029 unchanged; the fourth does not. Eight parts. This entry **amends D-029(a), (b), (d) and (e)** by name, **sharpens D-041(c) and D-043(h)** (two fetches, one answer), **adds one row to D-045(e)'s per-kind TTL table** and **adds one field to D-029(b)'s model**. D-024, D-028 and D-031 are unamended and the published tool count does not move. R-6 is unblocked; `tasks/T43.md` is written.

**Everything below that is a number or a quotation is `tasks/T48-recon.md`'s, fetched that day.** Nothing here was re-fetched, and nothing here rests on a claim the recon marked `[search]` except the decommissioning of the old EC zone, which is used only as a reason not to depend on it.

**(a) `registered: false` is earnable only on the SML/SMP route. A Peppol Directory miss is `null`, never `false`. This amends D-029(b) and D-029(d).** D-029(b) says *"`registered=False` is an honest value here … an authoritative SMP resolution that finds no participant is a definite 'not reachable on the network'"* and D-029(d) allows the Directory as a fallback. Each sentence is defensible alone; composed, they license a Directory miss to be reported as `false`, and **both operators say in writing that a Directory miss means nothing**. The Directory's own Introduction page: *"publication of Peppol participant information in an SMP happens **on a voluntary basis** … if your desired partner does not show up in here it doesn't mean that the entity is not in the Peppol Network!"* — and, in the same breath, the positive half: *"if an entity shows up in the Peppol Directory you can be sure that the participant is a participant of the Peppol Network."* DFØ says the same in Norwegian. Digdir, who operate ELMA, put the rule in one line: *"The only valid way to lookup an organization is the PEPPOL way of lookup via the SML."*

So the Directory is **sound for a positive and unsound for a negative**, and that asymmetry — not the route's rank — is what decides the field:

| `registered` | Earned by |
|---|---|
| `true` | a ServiceGroup 200 from the SMP the SML named; **or** a non-empty Directory match |
| `false` | **NXDOMAIN at the SML, or a 404 from the SMP the SML named. Nothing else, ever** |
| `null` | everything else — a resolver that did not answer, a NOERROR with no `Meta:SMP` record, an SMP 5xx or timeout, **and a Directory miss** |

Two consequences that are rules, not readings. **The Directory is consulted only when the SML/SMP route produced no answer at all** — not after an NXDOMAIN, which is already the answer, and not after a ServiceGroup, which is a better one. It is a repair for our own failure to reach the authoritative route, so it costs one request on a bad day and none on a good one, and the 2 queries/second ceiling is never approached. And **the Directory can only ever raise a `null` to a `true`** — it may fill `document_types` (the block says it did), and it may never lower anything. *Declined: reporting a Directory miss as `false` with a caveat in `notes`.* A caveat does not travel: `registered` is the field an agent branches on, and a `false` that means "an index we were told is incomplete did not list them" is the D-011 collapse in the one place where the cost of it is a caller skipping a statutory duty. *Declined: dropping the Directory fallback entirely* now that it cannot produce the negative. It still turns "we could not ask" into "yes" for a caller whose network blocks DNS but not HTTPS — see (c) — and a positive is the answer that lets a sender comply.

**(b) `provenance.source` names the SMP host that answered, read from the NAPTR, and a hardcoded host is a defect. This amends D-029(d).** D-029(a) states that ELMA is *the* Norwegian SMP; Digdir states in writing that this stopped being true: *"A norwegian organization may be registered to an other SMP. A lookup only in ELMA is no longer a valid way to lookup norwegian organizations in PEPPOL."* Measured: of **43** Norwegian participants resolved, **42 → `https://smp.elma-smp.no/`** and **1 → `https://smp.conta.no`** (`0192:837056942`), whose ServiceGroup answers keyless exactly like ELMA's. D-029(d)'s *"name in `provenance.source` which one answered"* is amended to **"which route *and which SMP* answered"**, and `provenance.source` is **derived at request time, never a constant** — the SMP host plus "via the Peppol SML" on the authoritative route, the Directory named as an index that may lag on the fallback. `smp_url` carries the NAPTR's base URL verbatim; `source_url` carries the exact URL fetched.

**One constant, one datum, and the difference is the whole ruling.** The **zone** is a constant, because it is the network's root and nothing derives it: `participant.sml.prod.tech.peppol.org`, from *Peppol SML v1.3.0* (valid 2025-11-01), with the record type NAPTR and the service name `Meta:SMP`. The **SMP host is data**, read fresh from that NAPTR on every uncached lookup. Both halves are load-bearing and the trap has two mouths:

1. **Hardcoding `smp.elma-smp.no`** works for 42 of 43 — which is exactly why an implementer does it and why the tests pass — and reports `registered: false` for a company that *is* registered. That is the worst available failure for a 2027 statutory answer and the inverse of the one D-045(f) predicted.
2. **Hardcoding the EC zone `edelivery.tech.ec.europa.eu`, or the `B-<md5>` CNAME form.** Every pre-2025 tutorial, library README and blog post uses one or both. The CNAME form is **dead** — NXDOMAIN on 6 of 6 combinations tested, and SML v1.3.0's own changelog says why (*"Switching from CNAME to U-NAPTR DNS records"*, *"Removed the CNAME Wildcard option"*); the EC zone still answered on 2026-09-08 and is scheduled for decommissioning at the Commission's discretion. Code written from a tutorial passes review, passes tests, and returns an NXDOMAIN that looks exactly like "not registered".

So: **the SMP base URL is never a constant, never inferred from the country, and never remembered across lookups** (see (d)). `grep -rn "elma-smp\|edelivery.tech.ec.europa.eu" src/registry_mcp/` returning anything outside a comment explaining this paragraph is a defect, and `tasks/T43.md` makes it a done-check.

**(c) The SML step buys `dnspython` as a sixth runtime dependency. DNS-over-HTTPS is refused.** The SML step reads a U-NAPTR record (RFC 3403); Python's standard library resolves names to addresses and cannot read a NAPTR at all, and `grep -rn "dnspython\|NAPTR\|resolver" src/` returns nothing. This is the one thing D-029 does not decide and an implementer must not.

**The cost is smaller than it looks, and this is checkable rather than argued.** `dnspython 2.8.0` is **already resolved and hash-pinned in `uv.lock`** (`:482`) — it arrives as `pydantic[email]`'s transitive, an extra nothing in this project selects — so promoting it to `[project].dependencies` adds no new resolution, no new hash and no new publisher to the tree; the wheel is `py3-none-any` (pure Python, no compiled extension) at 331 kB. The hash step needs nothing at all: `base64.b32encode(hashlib.sha256(pid.lower().encode()).digest()).rstrip(b"=")` reproduces the SML spec's own worked example (`0010:5798000000001` → `XUKHFQABQZIKI3YKVR2FHR4SNFA3PF5VPQ6K4TONV3LMVSY5ARVQ`) and Equinor's (`0192:923609016` → `XQK4T3FMTEZDUY5BOVJMTNAQ45N7E4TIBYHWFPGOT75BSP7UYG2A`) byte for byte, offline, in one line of stdlib. Only the DNS read needs the library.

*Declined: DNS-over-HTTPS through the existing `httpx`* — which is what the recon itself used, on a machine with no `dig` and no `dnspython`, and which the recon flagged as an expedient rather than a design. Four reasons, and the first is the one that decides it.

1. **Whose resolver.** Every DNS lookup trusts a resolver; the question is whose. `dnspython` uses **the operator's own** — `/etc/resolv.conf`, the same resolver every other program on that host uses, and the same trust model every Peppol access point uses to perform this exact walk in production. A hardcoded DoH endpoint substitutes a resolver **we** chose, belonging to a company with no relationship to the Peppol network, into the trust path of an answer a caller will act on under bokføringsloven § 10. Under D-018 we would then have to say so, and the honest sentence — *Google told us what the Peppol SML says* — is one nobody wants to publish in a provenance block. That reluctance is the argument.
2. **Exposure.** A DoH request carries the participant label in a URL query string to a third-party host: D-039's exposure category (URLs, access logs, `Referer`), the one D-045(e) refused GLEIF for Sweden over. This is **not** that rule's case — Norway does not set `id_may_be_personal` and the label is a hash — but a SHA-256 over a nine-digit space is a lookup key and not a secret, and the Directory publishes a bulk export of the whole participant list. DoH would hand a third party a legible log of every Norwegian company our callers ask about, in exchange for nothing.
3. **It does not win the locked-down case, which is its only practical argument.** Enterprises block DoH resolvers *deliberately* — defeating DNS-based filtering and logging is what DoH is for — so `dns.google` is **more** likely to be refused on a hardened network than the site's own resolver, which must work or the machine resolves nothing at all. And an egress allowlist would have to name a Google host in a company-register server's self-hosting documentation.
4. **It describes a DNS lookup as an HTTP fetch.** The SML step is DNS in the specification and DNS on the wire; routing it through an HTTP client to avoid one line in `pyproject.toml` is two shapes for one concept, which D-042(b) declined by name.

*Declined: a hand-rolled NAPTR query over `socket`.* Wire-format encoding, EDNS, truncation and TCP fallback is writing and then maintaining a resolver, which is what `dnspython` already is. *Declined: a DoH fallback when the system resolver fails.* A fallback that silently reroutes a statutory answer through a third party is worse than the `null` it avoids, because the caller cannot see that it happened; if it is ever wanted it is an opt-in with its own entry and its own provenance sentence. *Declined: a `REGISTRY_MCP_*_RESOLVER` variable naming nameserver addresses.* The operator's resolver is configured where every other program on the box reads it — `/etc/resolv.conf`, or the container runtime's `--dns` — and a second, registry-mcp-only place to configure DNS is a second mechanism for a dial the platform already has, which is the argument D-045(e) used against a second cache-TTL mechanism.

**What a self-hoster on a locked-down network sees — and it is the same picture either way, which is the last reason DoH buys nothing.** If the resolver answers, nothing is different. If it refuses, times out, SERVFAILs, or filters NAPTR, **no `false` is earned**: the block is present, `participant_id` is populated offline from the orgnr (D-029(c), unamended and now load-bearing), `registered: null`, `smp_url: null`, `document_types: []`, `can_receive_invoice: null`, and one `notes` sentence names the DNS step as the one that failed. The lookup itself still succeeds and the other blocks are unaffected (D-042(b)). The Directory fallback then runs over ordinary HTTPS — on precisely such a network it is often the only route open — and can raise that `null` to a `true`, never to a `false`. **The degraded answer is "we could not ask" or "yes", never "no"**, which is the only direction that cannot cause a breach.
